Trade between South India and Southeast Asia had started from the sixth century onwards. The growing geographical knowledge about these countries is reflected in the literature of the time. The peculiar features of the languages, dresses, etc., are mentioned in the books of the period, such as Harisena’s Brihatkatha-kosh. There are many stories about the adventures of Indian merchants in the magical waters of the area, which became the basis of the well-known story of Sindbad the Sailor. Indian merchants were organized in guilds, the most famous of them being the Manigramam and the Nandest, which had been active since early times. These guilds displayed a spirit of enterprise, engaging in retail and wholesale trade in many foreign countries. They also gave handsome grants to temples, which became centers of social and cultural life, and sometimes advanced money for trade. Many Indian traders settled in these countries, some of whom married local women. The priests followed the traders. In this way, both Buddhist and Hindu religious ideas spread to the region. The Buddhist temple of Borobudur in Java and the Hindu temple of Angkor Wat in Cambodia testify to the spread of both religions there. Some ruling families in the region became semi-Hinduized and welcomed trade and cultural relations with India. Indian culture mingled with local culture to establish new literary and cultural forms. Some observers believe that the material prosperity of Southeast Asia, the growth of civilization, and the establishment of large states were largely due to the introduction of Indian techniques of irrigated rice cultivation.

        The chief Indian port for sailing to Java, Sumatra, etc., was Tamralipti (Tamluk) in Bengal. In most of the stories of the period, merchants start for Suvarnadvipa (modern Indonesia) or Kataha (Kedah in Malaya) from Tamralipti. A fourteenth-century writer in Java speaks of people from Jambudvipa (India), Karnataka (South India), and Gaud (Bengal) coming in large numbers on big ships. Traders from Gujarat also took part in this trade.

        Due to its prosperity, China had become a main focus of trade in the Indian Ocean. The Chinese consumed enormous quantities of spices, which were imported from Southeast Asia and India. They also imported ivory, the best of which came from Africa, and glassware from West Asia. Additionally, they imported medicinal herbs, lac, incense, and various rare commodities. Generally, products from Africa and West Asia did not go beyond Malabar in South India. Nor did many Chinese ships venture beyond the Moluccas in Southeast Asia. Thus, both India and Southeast Asia served as important staging centers for trade between China and the countries of West Asia and Africa. Indian traders, especially from the Tamil country and Kalinga (modern Orissa and Bengal), played an active role in this trade, along with Persians, and later, the Arabs. Much of the trade to China was carried in Indian ships, with teakwood from Malabar, Bengal, and Burma providing the basis for a strong tradition of shipbuilding. The weather conditions were such that ships could not sail directly from the Middle East to China. They had to wait in ports for favorable winds, which blew from the west to the east before the monsoon and from east to west after it. Indian and Southeast Asian ports were preferred by merchants for this reason.

        The main seaport for foreign trade in China during this period was Canton, or Kanfu, as the Arab travelers called it. Buddhist scholars traveled from India to China by sea. Chinese chroniclers tell us that the number of Indian monks in the Chinese court at the close of the tenth and the beginning of the eleventh century was the highest in Chinese history. A Chinese account from a slightly earlier period states that the Canton river was full of ships from India, Persia, and Arabia. It mentions that in Canton itself, there were three Hindu temples where Indians resided. The presence of Indians in Chinese seas is also confirmed by Japanese records, which credit two Indians for introducing cotton into Japan.

        Indian rulers, particularly the Pala and Sena rulers of Bengal, and the Pallava and Chola rulers of South India, encouraged this trade by sending a series of embassies to the Chinese emperors. The Chola ruler, Rajendra I, sent a naval expedition against Malaya and the neighboring countries to counter their interference in the trade with China. Rajendra I’s embassy to China traveled in an Indian ship. Evidence suggests that many shipyards were located along the west coast, including in Gujarat. Thus, the growth of India’s foreign trade in the region was based on a strong maritime tradition, including shipbuilding, and the skill and enterprise of its traders.

        The Chinese trade was very favorable to the countries engaged in it, so much so that in the thirteenth century, the Chinese government tried to restrict the export of gold and silver from China. Indian ships gradually gave way to the larger and faster Chinese and Arab ships. Chinese ships were several stories high and could carry 600 passengers in addition to 400 soldiers. An important factor in the growth of Chinese ships was the use of the mariner’s compass, an invention that later traveled from China to the West. By this time, Indian science and technology were being left behind.

        Thus, India’s trade with Western areas and with Southeast Asia and China grew steadily. South India, Bengal, and Gujarat took the lead in this trade, contributing significantly to the wealth and prosperity of these areas.

    Condition of the People
        There was no decline in the high standard of Indian handicrafts such as textiles, gold and silver work, metallurgy, etc., during this period. Indian agriculture also continued to thrive.

        Many Arab travelers testify to the fertility of the soil and the skill of the Indian peasant.

        All the literary works of the period tell us that ministers, officials, and feudal chiefs lived in great ostentation and splendour. They copied the ways of kings, living in fine houses that were sometimes three to five stories high. They wore costly foreign apparel such as imported woolen clothes, Chinese silk, and costly jewels and ornaments made of gold and silver. They maintained large households with many women and a train of domestic servants. Whenever they went out, they were accompanied by many attendants. They assumed high-sounding titles, such as mahasamantadhipati, and had distinctive symbols, such as banners, decorated umbrellas, and yak-tails to whisk away flies.

        Big merchants also emulated the kings, and their lifestyles were sometimes quite royal. Of a millionaire (Xozésvare) in the Chalukyan empire, it is said that huge banners with ringing bells were hoisted over his house, and that he owned many horses and elephants. The main building was approached by a crystal staircase, and the temple had crystal floors and walls adorned with religious paintings containing a crystal image. Vaseupala and Tejahpala, who were ministers in Gujarat, are reputed to have been the richest merchants of their time.

        We cannot, however, assume from the above that there was prosperity all around. While foodstuffs were cheap, many poor people in the cities could not get enough to eat. The author of the Rajatarangini (written in Kashmir in the twelfth century) has them in mind when he says that, whereas the courtiers ate fried meat and drank cooled wine perfumed with flowers, the ordinary people had to be content with rice and stpala-saka (a wild vegetable of bitter taste). There are many stories about the hard lot of poor men and women, some of whom resorted to a life of robbery and plunder. As for the villages, where the large bulk of the population lived, we have to gather information about the life of the peasants from literary works, grants of land, inscriptions, etc. The commentators on Dharmashastras tell us that the rate of the revenue demand from the peasants was one-sixth of the produce, as before. However, from some of the grants, we learn of a large number of additional cesses, such as grazing tax, tax on ponds, etc. The peasants had to pay these taxes over and above the land-revenue. In addition, some of the grants gave the grantees the right to levy fixed or unfixed, proper or improper taxes on the peasants. The peasants also had to render forced labor (vishai). In some cases, as in central India and Orissa, we find some villages being given to Brahmins and other donees along with artisans, herdsmen, and cultivators who were tied to the soil like serfs in medieval Europe. In literary works, we hear of chiefs realizing money on every opportunity that offered itself. We are told of a Rajput chief who made money even from sparrows, dead birds, pig dung, and the shrouds of dead bodies. Another writer tells us of a village that was depopulated due to the actions of a chief (samania).

        To this may be added the frequent recurrence of famines and wars. In the wars, the destruction of water reservoirs, burning of villages, seizure by force of all the cattle or the grains stored in granaries, and destruction of cities were normal features, so much so that they were considered legitimate by the writers of the period.

        Thus, the growth of a society that may be called feudal increased the burdens on the common man.

    NATURE OF SOCIETY
        A number of important changes took place in Indian society during this period. One of these was the growing power of a class of people who were variously called semanta, randk, rdutia (Rajput), etc., by contemporary writers. Their origins were very different. Some were government officers who were increasingly paid not in cash but by assigning to them revenue-bearing villages. Others were defeated rajas and their supporters who continued to enjoy the revenue of limited areas. Still others were local hereditary chiefs or military adventurers who had carved out a sphere of authority with the help of armed supporters. Some were tribal or clan leaders. Thus, there was a hierarchy among them, but their actual position varied depending on the situation. Some of them were only village chiefs, some dominated a tract comprising a number of villages, while a few dominated an entire region. They constantly contended against each other and tried to enhance their sphere of authority and privileges.

        The revenue assignments (called jagir or fief) granted by a ruler to his officers and supporters were temporary in theory and were liable to be resumed whenever the ruler wanted. However, in practice, this was rarely done, except in the case of outright rebellion or disloyalty. According to current notions, it was a sin to deprive even a defeated ruler of his lands.

        As a result, the kingdoms of this period included large areas dominated by defeated and subordinate rulers who were constantly on the lookout for reasserting their independence. Within the territories of these rulers, various officers looked upon their assignments as hereditary fiefs. In the course of time, even various government offices began to be considered hereditary. We have seen earlier in a case from Bengal, where members of a family held the office of makamani for four generations. Similarly, most offices began to be considered the monopoly of a few families. The hereditary chiefs gradually assumed many of the functions of the government. They not only assessed and collected land revenue, but also assumed more and more administrative powers, such as the right to award punishments and exact fines on their own, powers that were previously considered royal privileges. They assumed the right to sublet their land to their followers without the prior permission of the ruler, thus increasing the number of people who drew sustenance from land without working on it themselves.

        Some historians call this type of society ‘feudal’, although features of European feudalism, such as vassalage, serfdom, and manors, did not exist in India. It is emphasized that the common feature between the two was that the society was dominated by a class of people who derived their income from the surplus produced by the peasants but did not work on the land themselves. Also, the position of the primary producers, the peasants, was a dependent one. A number of other historians prefer to call this society a ‘medieval society’. They consider feudalism to be the specific feature of ‘medieval’ European society, as we have mentioned earlier.

        Without delving further into this controversy, we may note that a new type of society grew in India from the eighth century onwards, emphasizing localism and sub-regionalism. This is important for understanding the political and cultural developments during this and the subsequent period.

    THE CASTE SYSTEM
        (a) Brahmans and Rajputs
            The caste system, which had been established much earlier, continued to be the basis of society. But there were important changes within the caste system. There was considerable strengthening of the position of the Brahmans. As we have seen, the ruler of Sind, who was himself a Brahman, and the rulers of Bengal and South India, invited Brahmans and gave large-scale revenue-free lands to them to settle down. These Brahmans not only expanded cultivation but also acted as local revenue officials, ministers, accountants, etc. Some of them also played an active role in military affairs. Education and the production of literary works were other important fields of their activities. The writers of the period appear to reflect the growing importance of the Brahmans by further exalting their position. Some argue that the ancient caste of Kshatriyas had disappeared, and the Vaishyas had sunk to the position of Shudras, leaving the Brahmans as the only dvija (twice-born or privileged) section in society. However, this view in the shastras did not necessarily reflect social reality. We have already referred to the existence of rich merchants, Hindu and Jain, and the rise of a powerful class of landed chiefs. In this context, we have the rise of a new section called the Rajputs. During this period, a large number of states were being ruled by Rajputs. These Rajputs were considered leaders of clans that dominated certain tracts of land and provided the core of the armed forces. The leaders of the clan, most of whom were related to the ruler by ties of blood, considered the state to be ruled jointly by them.

            There is a good deal of controversy among scholars about the origin of the Rajputs. Some consider them to be of mixed origin, some being descendants of foreigners such as Shakas, Hunas, etc., and indigenous tribes, even Brahmans. On the other hand, many Rajput clans, traditionally numbering thirty-six, trace their genealogy to the solar and lunar families of the Kshatriyas mentioned in the Mahabharata. Modern scholarship emphasizes the process by which people belonging to different social groups tried to legitimize their newly acquired power and position by being accorded the status of Kshatriyas. Sometimes a mixed Brahman-Kshatriya status was sought by claiming descent through a Brahman mother. Scholars consider this to be part of a complex process of social growth. Thus, in some areas of Rajasthan, tribal lands were colonized, and Brahmans, traders, and warriors settled on the land. In many areas, this was accompanied by introducing a superior type of economy based on irrigation through wells, bunds, etc., and the cultivation of superior crops. In the process, some of the cultivators became Rajputs, while others remained Shudras.

            What is called ‘Rajputization’ is accompanied by the growth of the agrarian economy, and also the acquisition of political power by some sections. The Brahmans played an important role in this process. Thus, there was the agniput legend, traced to the eleventh century, whereby the sage Vashishtha produced four Rajput clans—the Pratiharas, Solankis or Chalukyas, Parmars or Pawars, and Chauhans or Chahamans—from the sacrificial fire. During this period, Brahmans wrote many genealogies of ruling families, linking them to ancient Kshatriya families. Thus, the Gurjar-Pratiharas, reputed to originate from the Gurjar stock, were linked to Lakshman, who had acted as the door-keeper (pratihar) of Rama.

            This Brahman-Rajput alliance had many political and cultural consequences. The Rajputs, acting as champions of the newly expansionist Hinduism, symbolized their power by building grand temples and endowed them, along with Brahman priests, with large grants of lands, gifts, and endowments.

            Thus, caste (jaati) is not as rigid as has sometimes been believed: individuals and groups could rise in the varna scale, and they could also fall. Sometimes, it was difficult to classify new castes in the varna scale. An instance of this is the Kayastha caste, which begins to be mentioned more prominently from this period. It seems that originally, people from different castes, including Brahmans and Shudras, who worked in royal establishments, were called Kayasthas. In time, they emerged as a distinct caste. Hinduism was expanding rapidly during this period. It not only absorbed large numbers of Buddhists and Jains but also many indigenous tribes. Foreigners such as the Hunas were also Hinduized. These new sections formed new castes and sub-castes, with the tribals often continuing their own customs, marriage rituals, and even their tribal gods and goddesses, who were often made subordinate to Hindu gods. Thus, society and religion became more and more complex.

        (b) Shudras, Dalits, and Slaves
            According to the law-giver Yajnavalkya, it was possible for a Brahman to have food with his farmer, barber, milkman, and family friends. According to modern historian D.C. Sircar, an important feature of the caste system during this period was ‘the gradual elevation of the social position of the Shudras’. Although they were not allowed to study the Vedas, they became eligible for smarta rituals, such as those for birth, death, name-giving, etc. As agriculture expanded, many of the tribals were included in this category. The Jats, who were a pastoral nomadic tribe in Sind, gradually moved to the Punjab and became agriculturists and warriors. Although in the varna system they were classified as Shudras, they formed a higher section and considered themselves on par with the Rajputs.

            Marriages between the higher castes and the Shudras were frowned upon, but that they existed is shown by dubbing the marriage of a high-caste man with a lower-caste woman as anuloma (according to norms), and the marriage of a low-caste man with a high-caste woman as pratiloma (against norms). The origin of new castes, often consisting of professional groups such as potters, weavers, barbers, etc., or tribals, was explained as the result of such mixed marriages.

            As compared to the Shudras, the position of the Dalits seems to have deteriorated. The Dalits included those following professions such as scavenging, skinning dead animals, shoemaking, hunting, etc. These people were called antyaja or untouchables, and formed the fifth social grade, outside the four-fold varna system. They were often required to live away from areas inhabited by upper castes. There is even a discussion in the shastras on whether the shadow of an antyaja was polluting. In some parts of the country, the Chandals were required to hit a wooden board on a stick as they walked, so that upper-caste people would not come into contact with them. In most areas, these sections were not allowed to own cultivated land.

            Slavery also existed during the period. Prisoners of war and debtors unable to pay their debts could be sold into slavery. During famines, many farmers sold themselves or their wives and children for food. Women were also purchased for domestic work or company. However, there were no systematic slave raids as in the case of the Turks. In general, slaves were treated better than people from the antyaja or despised castes.

            Though slaves were not used for fighting, they could fight. We are told that if a slave saved his master’s life, he became free and was entitled to a share of his master’s property. A female slave bearing a child to her master also became free. In general, emancipation of a slave was considered a good deed, and there were rules prescribed for doing so.

    CONDITION OF WOMEN
        As in earlier periods, women were generally considered to be mentally inferior. Their duty was to obey their husbands blindly. A writer illustrates the wife’s duty of personal service towards her husband by stating that she should shampoo his feet and render him other services befitting a servant. However, the writer adds that the husband should follow the righteous path and be free from hatred and jealousy towards his wife. Women continued to be denied the right to study the Vedas. Additionally, the marriageable age for girls was lowered, which hindered their opportunities for higher education. The omission of references to women teachers in dictionaries written during the period reflects the poor state of higher education for women.

        However, from some dramatic works of the period, we find that court ladies and even the queen’s maids-in-waiting were capable of composing excellent Sanskrit and Prakrit verses. Various stories highlight the skill of princesses in the fine arts, especially in painting and music. Daughters of high officials, courtesans, and concubines were also known to be highly skilled in various arts, including poetry.

        As for marriage, the Smriti writers say that girls were to be given away by their parents between the ages of six and eight or between their eighth year and attaining puberty. Remarriage was allowed under certain conditions if the husband had deserted (i.e., was not heard of), died, adopted the life of a recluse, became impotent, or had become an outcaste.

        In general, women were distrusted. They were kept in seclusion, and their lives were regulated by male relatives—father, brother, husband, or son. However, within the home, they were honored. If a husband abandoned a wife, even if she was guilty of offensive behavior, she was entitled to maintenance. With the growth of property rights in land, women’s property rights also increased.

        To preserve family property, women were given the right to inherit the property of their male relatives. With some reservations, a widow was entitled to the entire estate of her husband if he died without a son. Daughters also had the right to inherit the properties of a widow. Thus, the growth of feudal society strengthened the concept of private property.

        The practice of Sati was made obligatory by some writers but condemned by others. According to an Arab writer, Sulaiman, the wives of kings sometimes burnt themselves on the funeral pyre of their husbands, but it was for them to exercise their option in the matter. It appears that with the practice of a large number of women being maintained by the leading chiefs, and with resultant disputes about property, the practice of Sati spread.

        Upper-class women lived in seclusion and were generally kept away from public view. However, there was no system of purdah or veiling of women. Abu Zaid, a tenth-century Arab traveler, noted that most Indian princes, while holding court, allowed their women to be seen unveiled by all men present, including foreigners. In Orissa and Kashmir, many women ruled as queens in their own right. Among them were Prabhavati Gupta of the Vakataka dynasty, who ruled for at least thirteen years as the mother of the Yewaraja, and Queen Didda, who ruled Kashmir for fifty years and withstood all intrigues against her.

        We have little information on the lives of ordinary women. They must have worked hard, side by side with their men, in addition to managing the household and tending children.

    DRESS, FOOD, AND AMUSEMENTS
        There were no significant changes in the style of dress for men and women during this period. The dhoti and sari remained the normal attire for men and women in most parts of the country. In North India, men wore jackets, and women wore bodices (cholis). Sculptures suggest that long coats, trousers, and shoes were worn by upper-class men in North India. According to Rajatarangini, Harsha introduced a general dress befitting a king in Kashmir, which included a long coat. It is said that a former chief minister, who wore a short coat, incurred the king’s displeasure. Woolen blankets were used in winter, while cotton was the material most commonly used. The upper classes also used silk cloth and fine muslin.

        Arab travelers testify to the fondness of both men and women for wearing ornaments. Both men and women wore gold bracelets and earrings, sometimes set with costly stones. A Chinese writer, Chau Ju Kua, says that in Gujarat, both men and women wore double earrings, close-fitting clothes, hoods on their heads, and red-colored shoes on their feet. Another famous traveler, Marco Polo, tells us that in Malabar, men and women wore only a loincloth, the king being no exception. He also notes that the profession of tailoring was unknown. Loincloths were also worn by men and women in Quilon. Despite their scanty clothing, the kings of southern India were fond of jewelry. According to Chau Ju Kua, the king of Malabar wore a cotton loincloth and was barefoot like his subjects, but when going out in a procession on an elephant, he wore a golden hat ornamented with pearls and gems, as well as golden armlets and anklets. Marco Polo says, “What this king wears in gold, gems, and pearls is worth more than a city’s ransom.”

        As far as food is concerned, vegetarianism appeared to have been the rule in many areas and among many sections of the population. However, the leading Smriti writer of the times describes at great length the occasions when the eating of meat was lawful. From this, it appears that peacock, horse, wild ass, wild cock, and wild pig were considered lawful food.

        Arab writers compliment the Indians for the absence of intoxicants among them. However, this appears to be an idealized picture. Literary works of the period contain many references to wine-drinking. Wine was consumed on ceremonial occasions, including marriages and feasts, and was also a popular outing among some classes of citizens. Even women in the king’s train indulged freely in wine. While some Smriti writers forbade wine-drinking for the three upper castes, others allowed it for the Kshatriyas and Vaishyas, with some exceptions.

        The literature of the time shows that the people of the towns were fun-loving. Apart from fairs and festivals, excursions to gardens and swimming parties were widely popular. Fights among various types of animals, such as rams and cocks, as well as wrestling bouts, were popular among the masses. The upper classes continued to enjoy activities like dicing, hunting, and a form of Indian polo, which was regarded as a royal pastime.

    EDUCATION, SCIENCE, AND RELIGIOUS LEARNING
        The system of education that had gradually developed in earlier periods continued during this period with little change. There was no concept of mass education at that time. People learned what they felt was necessary for their livelihood. Reading and writing were confined to a small section, mostly Brahmans and some upper-class groups, especially Kayasthas.

        Sometimes, temples made arrangements for higher education as well. Generally, students had to go to the house of a teacher or live with them to receive higher education. In such cases, students had to pay fees to their teacher or give them a gift at the end of their education. Students who were too poor to pay fees were expected to do personal service for the teacher. The main subjects studied were the various branches of the Vedas and grammar. Logic and philosophy were also studied. The study of politics, including political morality, was popular among the nobility. A notable contribution to this branch of study was Kamandaka’s Nitisara.

        The Kayasthas had their own system of teaching administration, including accountancy. Science, including mathematics, astronomy, and medicine, was also taught at many centers. The Arab astronomer, Abu Maashir of Balkh, studied at Banaras for ten years during the ninth century.

        The responsibility for educating a craft or profession was generally left to the guilds or individual families. For example, there is a detailed description of how a merchant carefully trained his son for the profession.

        More formal education, with greater emphasis on secular subjects, continued at some of the Buddhist vihāras (monasteries), with Nalanda in Bihar being the most famous. Other centers of learning included Vikramsila and Uddandapur, also in Bihar. These centers attracted students from distant places, including Tibet. Education for most of the residents was free, and the rulers made lavish grants of money and land to support these centers. For example, Nalanda had a grant of 200 villages.

        Kashmir was another important center of education. Many Saiva sects and centers of learning flourished during this period. Important mathas were set up in South India, such as in Madurai and Srirangam. These educational centers played a significant role in fostering discussion on religion and philosophy. They enabled ideas to flow freely across the country, strengthening India’s cultural unity.

        The growth of science in India slowed down during this period, and the country was no longer regarded as a leading center of scientific thought. Surgery declined because the dissection of dead bodies was considered only fit for people of low castes, and surgery became the profession of barbers. Astronomy was gradually overshadowed by astrology. However, some progress was made in mathematics. The Lilawari of Bhaskar II, written during this period, remained a standard text for a long time. Some advances were also made in medicine through the use of minerals, especially mercury. Many books were written on plant sciences and for the treatment of animals, such as horses and elephants. However, no method was found for breeding fine-quality horses, and India remained dependent on imports from Central Asia, including Arabia and Iran. With the conquest of these regions by Muslim rulers, the Indian rulers faced difficulties in securing a supply of good horses.

        The stagnation of Indian science during this period can be attributed to several factors. The growth of science is closely connected with the development of society as a whole. During this period, society became increasingly rigid and narrow, with setbacks in urban life and communication, accompanied by growing religious orthodoxy.

        Another reason was the tendency of Indians to isolate themselves from the main currents of scientific thought outside India. This is reflected in the writings of al-Biruni, a noted scientist and scholar from Central Asia who lived in India for about ten years during the early part of the eleventh century. Although al-Biruni admired Indian science and learning, he noted the insular attitude of the learned people of India, particularly the Brahmans. He said: “They are haughty, foolish, vain, self-conceited, and stolid. They are by nature niggardly in communicating that which they know, and they take the greatest possible care to withhold it from men of another caste among their own people, still more from any foreigners. According to their belief, no other created beings besides them have any knowledge of science whatsoever.”

    Revival Movements and Beliefs
        The period is marked by a revival and expansion of Hinduism, accompanied by a continued decline of Buddhism and Jainism. Not only were the tenets of Buddhism and Jainism challenged at the intellectual level, but on some occasions, Buddhist and Jain monks were persecuted. According to tradition, the Pandya king at Madurai executed large numbers of Jain monks at the instance of a Saivite preacher, Sambandar. In some instances, their temples were also taken over. Thus, the temple of Puri was once a Buddhist temple, and the temple near the Qutub Minar had once been a Jain temple before being converted into a Vishnu temple. While it is unclear how widespread such instances were, even if they were few and far between, they can hardly be defended. However, these actions were not part of a misguided religious philosophy of temple-destruction, as seen with the early Arab and Turkish invaders later on.

        During this period, Buddhism gradually became confined to eastern India. The Pala rulers were patrons of Buddhism, but the decline of Pala power after the tenth century dealt a blow to Buddhism in the region. However, even more serious were the internal developments within Buddhism. Buddha had preached a practical philosophy, with a minimum of priesthood and speculation about God. Over time, this worship became more elaborate, with a belief that a worshiper could attain their desires by uttering magical words (mantras) and performing various mystic gestures. It was believed that these practices, along with various austerities and secret rites, could lead to supernatural powers, such as the ability to fly, become invisible, or see things at a distance. Humanity has long yearned for control over nature in such ways, and many of these desires have been fulfilled through modern science. Many Hindu yogis also adopted these practices, with Gorakhnath being the most famous among them. The followers of Gorakhnath were called Nath-Panthis, and at one time, they were popular all over northern India. Many of these yogis belonged to lower castes and denounced the caste system and the privileges claimed by Brahmins. The path they preached was called tantra, which was open to all, irrespective of caste distinctions.

        Thus, Buddhism did not so much decline as it assumed forms that made it indistinguishable from Hinduism.

        Jainism continued to be popular, particularly among trading communities. The Chalukyan rulers of Gujarat patronized Jainism. During this time, some of the most magnificent Jain temples, such as the Dilwara temple at Mt. Abu, were built. The Paramara rulers of Malwa also built many large images of Jain saints and of Mahavira, who began to be worshipped as a god. The magnificent Jainalayas (temples) built in various parts also acted as resting places for travelers. In southern India, Jainism reached its peak during the ninth and tenth centuries. The Ganga rulers of Karnataka were great patrons of Jainism. During this period, many Jain basadis (temples) and sahasramedhas (pillars) were set up in different regions. The colossal image at Shravanabelagola was set up during this time. The statue, about 18 meters high and carved out of granite rock, shows the saint standing and practicing rigid austerities, unmindful of the snakes coiled around his feet and the anthills that had grown up around him. The Jain doctrine of the four gifts—learning, food, medicine, and shelter—helped make Jainism popular among the people. Over time, the growing rigidity of Jainism and the loss of royal patronage led to its decline.

        The revival and expansion of Hinduism took many forms. Shiva and Vishnu became the chief gods, and magnificent temples were built to proclaim their supremacy. In the process, many local gods and goddesses, including those of tribals who had been Hinduized, became subordinate or their consorts. In eastern India, the consorts—Tara, the consort of Buddha, and Durga and Kali, the consorts of Shiva—became themselves the chief objects of worship. Nevertheless, the rise of the worship of Shiva and Vishnu signified the growth of cultural synthesis. Thus, in an era of disintegration, religion played a positive part. However, the religious revival also increased the power and arrogance of the Brahmins, leading to a series of popular movements that targeted the Brahmins and emphasized human equality and freedom.

        We have already referred to the growth of tantrism in North India, where anyone, irrespective of caste, could be enrolled. But far more important and broad-based was the growth of the bhakti movement in South India. The bhakti movement was led by a series of popular saints called Nayanmars and Alvars. These saints rejected austerities and viewed religion not as a matter of cold, formal worship but as a living bond based on love between the god and the worshiper. The chief objects of their worship were Shiva and Vishnu, and they spoke and wrote in Tamil, a language understood by everyone. These saints traveled from place to place, spreading their message of love and devotion. Some of them belonged to lower classes, and there was even a woman saint, Andal. Almost all of them disregarded caste inequalities, though they did not try to oppose the caste system outright. The lower castes had been excluded from Vedic scholarship and worship, but the path of bhakti advocated by these saints was open to all, irrespective of caste.

        The bhakti movement not only brought many adherents of Buddhism and Jainism back to Hinduism, but it also won over many tribals. A series of acharyas, led by Nathamuni, collected and systematized the teachings of the Alvars and declared them equivalent to the Vedas. These early saints and their writings began to be worshipped in temples, and a whole set of rituals and ceremonies were elaborated. Many of these are still followed today.

        Many of the tribals from hilly areas became settled agriculturists in river valleys. These areas were often held by Brahmins, who introduced new agricultural techniques, or by temples where tribal gods were assimilated as supporters of Vishnu or Shiva. Expanded agriculture and new temple rituals strengthened the position of rulers and local states, such as the Chola state. The temples also strengthened the position of Brahmins, who supervised the temples and received rich grants of land from the rulers. Thus, the bhakti movement had far-reaching economic and social consequences as well.

        Another popular movement that arose during the twelfth century was the Lingayat or Vir Saiva movement. Its founders, Basava and his nephew Channabasava, lived at the court of the Kalachuri kings of Karnataka. They established their faith after bitter disputes with the Jains. The Lingayats are worshippers of Shiva and strongly opposed the caste system. They rejected fasts, feasts, pilgrimages, and sacrifices. In the social sphere, they opposed child marriage and allowed the remarriage of widows.

        Thus, both in South and North India, the revival and expansion of Hinduism took two forms—renewed emphasis on the Vedas and Vedic worship, on the one hand, accompanied by a powerful literary and intellectual movement, and, on the other, a popular movement based on tantra in North India and bhakti in South India. Both tantra and bhakti disregarded caste inequalities and were open to all.

        At the intellectual level, the most serious challenge to Buddhism and Jainism came from Sankara, who reformulated Hindu philosophy. Sankara was born in Kerala, probably in the ninth century. His life is shrouded in obscurity, and many legends have grown around it. Persecuted by the Jains, it is said that he undertook a triumphant visit to North India, where he defeated his opponents in debates. His victory was completed by a warm welcome from the king on his return to Madurai and the banishment of the Jains from the court.

        Sankara’s philosophy is called Advaita Vedanta, or the doctrine of non-dualism. According to Sankara, God and the created world are one. The differences between them are apparent but not real, arising from ignorance, with maya being a part of it. The way to salvation was through devotion to God, reinforced by the knowledge that God and all created beings are one and the same. Thus, Sankara upheld the Vedas as the fountainhead of true knowledge.

        The path of knowledge proposed by Sankara could be followed by only a few. Sankara did not reject the path of bhakti, through which the devotee merges with God, but for this, the heart had to be purified through jnana (knowledge). It could not, therefore, influence the masses. The acharyas from Nathamuni onwards were all orthodox Brahmins who argued that the path of bhakti was open only to the three upper castes and required dutifully following rituals prescribed by Brahmins and studying the scriptures.

        In the eleventh century, another famous acharya, Ramanuja, tried to assimilate bhakti with the Vedic tradition. He argued that, in order to attain salvation, the grace of God was more important than knowledge of Him. Ramanuja emphasized that the path of Prapatti (total reliance on or surrender to God) was open to all, including the Shudras and Dalits. Thus, Ramanuja tried to build a bridge between the popular bhakti movement and the upper-caste Vedic movement.

        The tradition established by Ramanuja was followed by several thinkers, such as Madhvacharya (tenth century), and in North India, by Ramananda, Vallabhacharya, and others. In this way, bhakti in its popular form became acceptable to all sections of Hindu society by the early sixteenth century.


- The Age of Conflict (Circa 1000-1200)
    The period from 1000 to 1200 saw rapid changes in both West and Central Asia, as well as in northern India. It was these developments that led to the incursion of the Turks into northern India, eventually establishing their rule toward the end of the period.

    By the end of the ninth century, the Abbasid caliphate was in decline. Its place was taken by a series of states ruled by Islamized Turks. The Turks had entered the Abbasid empire during the ninth century as palace-guards and mercenary soldiers. Soon, they emerged as king-makers. As the power of the central government declined, provincial governors began assuming independent status, though for some time, the fiction of unity was maintained by the caliph formally granting the title of aiz-al-temra (Commander of Commanders) to generals who were able to carve out separate spheres of authority. These new rulers initially assumed the title of amir and later that of sultan.

    The continuous incursion of Turkish tribesmen from Central Asia, the mercenary character of the Turkish soldiers who were prepared to switch loyalties and abandon an unsuccessful ruler without much thought, the strife between different Muslim sects, and between different regions made this a restless period. Empires and states rose and fell in rapid succession. In this situation, only a bold warrior and leader, adept in both warfare and withstanding intrigues, could rise to prominence.

    The Turkish tribesmen brought with them the habit of ruthless plunder. Their main mode of warfare consisted of rapid advances and retreats, lightning raids, and attacking any loose body of stragglers. They could do this because of the excellent quality of their horses and their hardihood, allowing them to cover incredible distances on horseback.

    Meanwhile, the breakup of the Gurjara-Pratihara empire led to a phase of political uncertainty in northern India, and a new phase of struggle for domination. As a result, little attention was paid to the emergence of aggressive, expansionist Turkish states on the northwestern border of India and in West Asia.

    Kabul, Qandahar, and the neighboring area to the south called Zabul or Zamindawar, were considered parts of al-Hind (India) until the end of the ninth century. The area had many Buddhist and Hindu shrines, the most important being the 53.5 m colossal statue of Buddha at Bamiyan, with caves for the residence of a thousand monks. The area up to the river Oxus was ruled by many dynasties, some of them claiming descent from Kanishka. These kingdoms, backed by a mixed population of local tribes like the Hunas, Furks, Iranians, and Indians (such as Bhati Rajputs), offered stiff resistance to the Arab efforts to enter the area for tribute, plunder, and slaves. Consequently, there was continuous skirmishing on both sides of the border.

    The Ghaznavids
        Towards the end of the ninth century, Trans-Oxiana, Khurasan, and parts of Iran were being ruled by the Samanids, who were Iranians by descent. The Samanids had to battle continually with non-Muslim Turkish tribesmen on their northern and eastern frontiers. It was during this struggle that a new type of soldier, the ghazi, was born. The battle against the Turks, most of whom worshipped the forces of nature and were heathens in the eyes of the Muslims, was a struggle for religion as well as for the safety of the state. Hence, the ghazi was as much a missionary as a fighter. He acted as a loose auxiliary to the regular armies and made up for his pay by plunder. It was the resourcefulness of the ghazi and his willingness to undergo great privations for the cause that enabled these infant Muslim states to hold their own against the heathen Turks. In time, many Turks became Muslims, but the struggle against renewed incursions of non-Muslim Turkish tribes continued. The Islamized Turkish tribes would later emerge as the greatest defenders and crusaders of Islam, though the love of plunder went hand-in-hand with the defense of Islam.

        Among the Samanid governors was a Turkish slave, Alp-Tigin, who, in time, established an independent kingdom with its capital at Ghazni. The Samanid kingdom soon ended, and the Ghaznavids took over the task of defending Islamic lands from the Central Asian tribesmen.

        It was in this context that Mahmud ascended the throne (998–1030) at Ghazni. Mahmud is considered a hero of Islam by medieval Muslim historians because of his stout defense against the Central Asian Turkish tribal invaders. The ghazi spirit, therefore, further increased during his reign. Secondly, Mahmud was closely associated with the renaissance of the Iranian spirit, which grew rapidly during this period. The proud Iranians had never accepted the Arabic language and culture. The Samanid state had also encouraged the Persian language and literature. A high watermark in the Iranian renaissance was reached with Firdausi’s Shah Namah. Firdausi was the poet laureate at the court of Mahmud. He transported the struggle between Iran and Turan to mythical times and glorified the ancient Iranian heroes. There was a resurgence of Iranian patriotism, and Persian language and culture became the language and culture of the Ghaznavid empire, so much so that Mahmud himself claimed descent from the legendary Iranian king, Afrasiyab. Thus, the Turks became not only Islamized but also Persianized. It was this culture that they were to bring with them to India two centuries later.

        While Mahmud played an important role in the defense of the Islamic states against the Turkish tribes and in the Iranian cultural renaissance, in India, his memory is that of a plunderer and destroyer of temples. Mahmud is said to have made seventeen raids into India. The initial raids were directed against the Hindushahi rulers, who at the time held Peshawar and the Punjab. Their capital was at Udbhanda (Waihind, Peshawar). The Hindushahi rulers had been quick to recognize the danger of the rise of an aggressive, expansionist state on their southwestern border. The Hindushahi ruler, Jayapala, had, in alliance with the displaced Samanid governor of Ghazni, the Bhati ruler of the area around Multan, and the amir of Multan, invaded Ghazni. However, he suffered a defeat, and the coalition he built collapsed. In retaliation, the Ghaznavid ruler laid waste the area up to Kabul and Jalalabad.

        Around 990-91, under Sabuk-Tigin, the Shahis suffered a serious defeat. Following this, the provinces of Kabul and Jalalabad were annexed to Ghazni. As a prince, Mahmud had taken part in these battles. After his accession to the throne (998), he resumed the offensive against the Shahis. The Shahi ruler, Jayapala, had, in the meantime, strengthened his position by bringing Lohavar (Lahore) under his control. Thus, the Shahi rule extended from Peshawar to Punjab.

        In a furious battle near Peshawar in 1001, Jayapala was again defeated. Mahmud advanced to the Shahi capital and thoroughly ravaged it. Peace was made by ceding the territory west of the Indus to Mahmud. Soon after, Jayapala died and was succeeded by his son, Anandpala. According to some later accounts, Jayapala entered a funeral pyre following his defeat because he felt he had disgraced himself. The story that he had been taken prisoner by Mahmud and then released seems doubtful.

        Despite these setbacks, the Shahis were still strong enough to offer serious resistance to Mahmud’s efforts to penetrate into the Punjab. Mahmud also had to counter the attacks of non-Muslim Turks from Central Asia. However, in a decisive battle near the Indus in 1009, Anandpala was defeated, and Mahmud devastated his new capital, Nandana, in the Salt Range, and overran his fort called Nagarkot (wrongly confused with Nagarkot in Himachal, which Mahmud never reached). Anandpala was allowed to rule from Lahore as a feudatory for some time. But in 1015, Mahmud advanced to Lahore, plundered it, and ousted Anandpala. Soon, Ghaznavid territories extended up to the river Jhelum. Earlier, the Muslim kingdom of Multan had also been overrun. However, an attack on Kashmir by Mahmud in 1015 was foiled due to weather conditions.

        Thus, the struggle against the Shahis was prolonged, and the Shahis put up stout resistance. In this struggle, the Shahis were supported only by the Muslim ruler of Multan, who had been harassed by slave-taking raids from Ghazni and belonged to a sect which Mahmud considered heretical, and hence an enemy. It is noteworthy that apparently none of the Rajput rulers came to the aid of the Shahis, although in order to exaggerate the scale of Mahmud’s victory, the seventeenth-century historian, Ferishta, mentions that many Rajput rulers, including those from Delhi, Ajmer, and Kanauj, aided Jayapala in 1001. However, Ajmer had not been founded by then, and Delhi (Dhillika) was a small state. Likewise, the Gurjara-Pratiharas of Kanauj, whose sway had extended over Thanesar at one time, were in a much weakened condition. Thus, the Shahis fought virtually alone.

        By 1015, Mahmud was poised for an attack on the Indo-Gangetic valley. During the next half a dozen years, Mahmud launched a series of expeditions into the Indo-Gangetic plains. These raids were aimed at plundering the rich temples and towns that had amassed wealth over generations. The plundering of this wealth also enabled him to continue his struggle against his enemies in Central Asia. He also did not want to give time to the princes in India to regroup and combine against him. Mahmud’s raids into India alternated with battles in Central Asia. For his plundering raids into India, the ghazis came in handy. Mahmud also posed as a great destroyer of images for the glory of Islam. From the Punjab, Mahmud raided Thanesar, the old capital of Harsha. His most daring raids were against Kanauj in 1018 and Somnath in Gujarat in 1025. In the campaign against Kanauj, he sacked and plundered both Mathura and Kanauj. The following year, he invaded Kalinjar in Bundelkhand and returned loaded with fabulous riches. He was able to do all this with impunity due to the fact that no strong state existed in north India at that time. No attempt was made by Mahmud to annex any of these states.

        Between 1020 and 1025, Mahmud was engaged in Central Asian affairs. In 1025, he made a plan for raiding Somnath, which had a fabulously rich temple and attracted lakhs of pilgrims. It was also a rich port. The objective was also to create a sense of awe and shock among the Rajputs because he marched via Multan and Jaisalmer with a regular cavalry of 30,000. Meeting light opposition on the way, he reached Somnath. The commander of the city fled at his approach, but the citizens put up stout resistance. Mahmud broke the Sivalingam and ordered parts of it brought back with him to Ghazni. Evading the attempts of some Rajput rulers to block him on his way back, he had to counter the marauding bands of Jats in Sind. He returned to Ghazni loaded with immense wealth.

        It is difficult to say whether Mahmud’s attack on Somnath presaged an attack on Rajasthan and Gujarat. Mahmud returned the following year to punish the Jats who had harried him on his return to Ghazni. He died at Ghazni in 1030.

        It is incorrect to dismiss Mahmud as just a raider and plunderer.

        The Ghaznavid conquest of Punjab and Multan completely changed the political situation in North India. The Turks had crossed the chain of mountains defending India in the northwest and could make deeper incursions into the Gangetic heartland at any time. The reason they were not able to extend their conquests into the area for the next 150 years was due to the rapid changes that took place in Central Asia as well as in North India during this period.

        Following the death of Mahmud, a powerful empire, the Seljuk Empire, came into being. The Seljuk Empire included Syria, Iran, and Trans-Oxiana, and contended with the Ghaznavids for control of Khurasan. In a famous battle, Masud, the son of Mahmud, was completely defeated and had to flee to Lahore for refuge. The Ghaznavid Empire now shrank to Ghazni and the Punjab. Although the Ghaznavids continued to make plundering raids into the Gangetic Valley and Rajputana, they were no longer in a position to pose a serious military danger to India. Simultaneously, several new states arose in North India that could counter the Ghaznavid raids.

    The Rajput States
        The rise of a new section called the Rajputs and the controversy about their origins have already been mentioned. With the break-up of the Pratihara Empire, a number of Rajput states came into existence in North India. The most important of these were the Gahadavalas of Kanauj, the Paramaras of Malwa, and the Chauhans of Ajmer. There were also smaller dynasties in different parts of the country, such as the Kalachuris in the area around modern Jabalpur, the Chandellas in Bundelkhand, the Chalukyas of Gujarat, the Tomars of Delhi, etc. Bengal remained under the control of the Palas and, later, the Senas. The Gahadavalas of Kanauj gradually squeezed the Palas out of Bihar.

        At its height, the Gahadvala kingdom extended from Mongyr in Bihar to Delhi. The greatest ruler in the dynasty was Govind Chandra, who ruled in the first half of the twelfth century. He made Kanauj his capital, with Banaras remaining a second capital. Persian sources of the time call Govind Chandra the greatest ruler of Hindustan. The Gahadavalas are reputed to be the greatest defenders against the continued Ghaznavid raids into the doab. Govind Chandra was succeeded by Jai Chandra, who had to contend with the rising power of the Chauhans.

        The Chauhans, who had served under the rulers of Gujarat, established their capital at Nadol towards the end of the tenth century. The greatest ruler in the dynasty was, perhaps, Vigraharaj, who captured Chittor, established Ajmer (Ajayameru), and made it his capital. He built a Sanskrit College at Ajmer, and the Anasagar lake there. Like the Gahadavalas, the Chauhans, too, offered stout resistance to the Ghaznavid raids. Vigraharaj captured Delhi (Dhillika) from the Tomars in 1151 but allowed them to rule it as feudatories. Vigraharaj also came into conflict with the Paramaras of Malwa, where the most famous ruler, known in legend, was Bhoja. Both Vigraharaj and Bhoja were patrons of poets and scholars. Vigraharaj himself wrote a Sanskrit drama. Bhoja had to fight his neighbours to the north as well as to the south. He is credited with writing books on philosophy, poetics, yoga, and medicine.

        The most famous among the Chauhan rulers was Prithviraj III, who ascended the throne at the young age of eleven in or about 1177, but took the reins of administration in his hands when he was sixteen. He immediately embarked on a vigorous policy of expansion and brought many of the smaller Rajput states under his sway. However, he was not successful in his struggle against the Chalukyan ruler of Gujarat. This forced him to move towards the Ganga Valley. He led an expedition in Bundelkhand against its capital, Mahoba. It was in this struggle that the famous warriors, Alha and Udal, lost their lives. The Chandel ruler of Mahoba is said to have been supported in this struggle by Jai Chandra of Kanauj. The Gahadavalas had also contested the attempt of the Chauhans to control Delhi and the Punjab. It was these rivalries which made it impossible for the Rajput rulers to join hands to oust the Ghaznavids from the Punjab.

        The basis of Rajput society was the clan. Every clan traced its descent from a common ancestor, real or imaginary. The clans generally dominated a compact territory. Sometimes, these settlements were based on units of 12, 24, 48, or 84 villages. The chief would allot land in the villages to his sub-chiefs, who, in turn, would allot it to individual Rajput warriors for the maintenance of their family and horses. Attachment to land, family, and honor (maan) was a characteristic of the Rajputs. Each Rajput state was supposed to be ruled over by the rana or rawat in conjunction with his chiefs, who were generally his blood brothers. Though their fiefs were supposed to be held at the pleasure of the ruler, the Rajput notion of sanctity of land did not permit their resumption by the ruler, except in special circumstances, such as rebellion, absence of an heir, etc.

        The Rajput organization of society had both advantages and disadvantages. One advantage was the sense of brotherhood and egalitarianism which prevailed among the Rajputs. But the same trait made it difficult to maintain discipline among them. Feuds which continued for several generations were another weakness of the Rajputs. But their basic weakness was their tendency to form exclusive groups, each claiming superiority over the others. They were not prepared to extend the sense of brotherhood to non-Rajputs. This led to a growing gap between the Rajput ruling groups and the people, most of whom were non-Rajputs. The Rajputs form only about ten percent of the population in Rajasthan even today. The proportion of the Rajputs to the total population of the areas they dominated could not have been much higher during the eleventh and twelfth centuries.

        The Rajputs treated war as a sport. This and the struggle for land and cattle led to continuous warfare among the various Rajput states. The ideal ruler was one who led his armies after celebrating the Dussehra festival to invade the territories of his neighbors. The people, both in the villages and in the cities, suffered the most from this policy.

        Most of the Rajput rulers of the time were champions of Hinduism, though some of them also patronized Jainism. They gave rich donations and grants of land to Brahmins and temples. The Rajput rulers stood forth as protectors of the privileges of the Brahmins and of the caste system. Thus, the system of charging a lower rate of land revenue for Brahmins continued in some Rajput states till their merger in the Indian Union. In return for these and other concessions, the Brahmins were prepared to recognize the Rajputs as descendants of the old lunar and solar families of the kshatriyas, which were believed to have become extinct.

        The period after the eighth century, and particularly between the tenth and twelfth centuries, may be regarded as marking a climax in temple building activity in North India. Some of the most magnificent temples that we have today can be traced back to this period. The style of temple construction which came into prominence was called the nagara. Though found almost all over India, the main centres of construction in this style were in North India and the Deccan. Its main characteristic feature was the tall curved spiral roof over the garbhagriha or the deity room (deul). The main room was generally square, though projections could be made from each of its sides. An anteroom (mandapa) was added to the sanctum sanctorum and sometimes the temple was enclosed by high walls which had lofty gates. The most representative temples of this type are the group of temples at Khajuraho in Madhya Pradesh and at Bhubaneshwar in Orissa. The Parsvanatha temple, the Visvanatha temple, and the Kandarya Mahadeo temple at Khajuraho illustrate this style in its richest and most finished form. The rich and elaborate carvings on the walls of the temples show that the art of sculpture had attained its height. Most of these temples were built by the Chandellas, who ruled the area from the beginning of the ninth to the end of the thirteenth century.

        In Orissa, the most magnificent examples of temple architecture of the time are the Lingaraja temple (eleventh century) and the Sun temple of Konark (thirteenth century). The famous Jaganatha temple at Puri also belongs to this period.

        A large number of temples were built at various other places in North India—Mathura, Banaras, Dilwara (Abu), etc. Like the temples in South India, temples in North India also tended to become more and more elaborate. They were the centers of social and cultural life. Some of them, such as the temple of Somnath, became extremely wealthy. They ruled over many villages and took part in business activities. In addition to temples, the Rajput rulers also built many fine palaces, powerful forts, and works of public benefit, such as stepped wells (zolis), bunds, etc.

        The Rajput rulers also patronized arts and letters. Many books and plays were written in Sanskrit during the period under their patronage. Vastupala, the famous minister of the Chalukyan ruler Bhima in Gujarat, was a writer and a patron of scholars and the builder of the beautiful Jain temple at Mt. Abu. Ujjain and Dhara, the capitals of the Paramara rulers, were famous centres for Sanskrit learning. Many works were written in Apabhramsha and Prakrit, which represented the languages of the region. The Jain scholars made significant contributions in this direction, the most famous among them being Hemachandra, who wrote both in Sanskrit and Apabhramsha. With the revival of Brahmanism, Sanskrit supplanted Apabhramsha and Prakrit among the upper classes. However, literature in these languages, which were closer to the spoken languages, continued to be produced. The modern North Indian languages, such as Hindi, Bengali, and Marathi, began to emerge out of these popular languages during this period.

    The Turkish Conquest of North India
        After the Ghaznavid Conquest of the Punjab, two distinct patterns of relations between Muslims and Hindus emerged. One was the lure for plunder, which led to raids into the Gangetic valley and Rajputana by the successors of Mahmud. The rulers of the Rajput states put up spirited resistance against these raids and won victories against the Turks on several occasions. But the Ghaznavid state was no longer very powerful, and the gaining of several victories against it in local battles made the Rajput rulers more complacent.

        At the second level, Muslim traders were allowed and even welcomed in the country since they helped strengthen and augment India’s trade with Central and West Asian countries, thereby increasing the income of the state. Colonies of Muslim traders sprang up in some towns in north India. In the wake of these traders came a number of Muslim religious preachers called Sufis to Punjab. The Sufis preached the gospel of love, faith, and dedication to the one God. While they primarily directed their preachings towards the Muslim settlers, they also influenced some Hindus. Thus, a process of interaction between Islam and Hindu religion and society began. Lahore became a center of Arabic and Persian languages and literature. Hindu generals, such as Tilak, a barber by caste, commanded the Ghaznavid armies, which also recruited Hindu (Jat) soldiers.

        These two processes might have continued indefinitely but for another large-scale change in the political situation in Central Asia. Towards the middle of the twelfth century, another group of Turkish tribesmen, who were partly Buddhist and partly pagan, shattered the power of the Seljuk Turks. In the resulting vacuum, two new powers rose to prominence: the Khwarazmian empire based in Iran and the Ghurid empire based in Ghur, northwest Afghanistan. The Ghurids had started as vassals of Ghazni but soon threw off their yoke. The power of the Ghurids increased under Sultan Alauddin, who earned the title of “the world burner” (Jahan-Soz) because, in the middle of the twelfth century, he ravaged and burned Ghazni to the ground in revenge for the treatment of his brothers there. The rising power of the Khwarazmian empire severely limited the Ghurids’ ambitions in Central Asia. Khurasan, which was the bone of contention between the two, was soon conquered by Khwarazm Shah. This left the Ghurids with no option but to look for expansion towards India.

        In 1173, Shahabuddin Muhammad (1173–1206), also known as Muizzuddin Muhammad bin Sam, ascended the throne at Ghazni, while his elder brother ruled at Ghur. Proceeding by way of the Gomal pass, Muizzuddin Muhammad conquered Multan and Uchch. In 1178, he attempted to penetrate Gujarat by marching across the Rajputana desert, but the Gujarat ruler completely routed him in a battle near Mount Abu, and Muizzuddin Muhammad was lucky to escape alive. He now realized the necessity of creating a suitable base in Punjab before venturing on the conquest of India. Accordingly, he launched a campaign against the Ghaznavid possessions in the Punjab. By 1190, Muizzuddin Muhammad had conquered Peshawar, Lahore, and Sialkot, and was poised for a thrust towards Delhi and the Gangetic doab.

        Meanwhile, events had not been standing still in north India. The Chauhan power had been steadily growing. The Chauhan rulers had defeated and killed a large number of Turks who had tried to invade Rajasthan, most probably from the Punjab side. They had also captured Delhi (called Dhillika) from the Tomars around the middle of the century. The expansion of the Chauhan power towards the Punjab brought them into conflict with the Ghaznavid rulers of the area.

    The Battle of Tarain
        Thus, a battle between these two ambitious rulers, Muizzuddin Muhammad and Prithviraj, was inevitable. The conflict started with rival claims for Tabarhinda. In the battle fought at Tarain in 1191, the Ghurid forces were completely routed, with Muizzuddin Muhammad’s life being saved by a young Khalji horseman. Prithviraj now pushed on to Tabarhinda and conquered it after a twelve-month siege. Little attempt was made by Prithviraj to oust the Ghurids from Punjab. Perhaps he felt that this was another of the recurrent Turkish raids and that the Ghurid ruler would be content to rule over Punjab. This gave Muizzuddin Muhammad time to regroup his forces and make another bid for India the following year. He rejected the proposal, said to be made by Prithviraj, to leave Punjab under the possession of the Ghurid ruler.

        The second battle of Tarain in 1192 is regarded as one of the turning points in Indian history. Muizzuddin Muhammad had made careful preparations for the contest. It is said that he marched with 1,206,000 men, including a force of heavy cavalry fully equipped with steel coats and armor, and 10,000 mounted archers. It is not correct to think that Prithviraj was negligent in his affairs and only realized the situation when it was too late. While Skanda, the general of the last victorious campaign, was engaged elsewhere, Prithviraj soon realized the nature of the Ghurid threat and appealed to all the rajas of northern India for help. Many rajas sent contingents to help him, but Jaichandra, the ruler of Kanauj, stayed away. The legend that this was because Prithviraj had abducted Jaichandra’s daughter, Sanyogita, who was in love with him, is not accepted by many historians. The story was written much later as a romance by the poet Chand Bardai and includes many improbable events. As we have seen, there had been an old rivalry between the two states, so it is not surprising that Jaichandra stayed away.

        Prithviraj is said to have fielded a force of 300,000, including a large body of cavalry and 300 elephants. The strength of the forces on both sides may have been exaggerated. The numerical strength of the Indian forces was probably greater, but the Turkish army was better organized and led. The battle was mainly a clash of cavalry. The superior organization, skill, and speed of movements of the Turkish cavalry and their mounted archers and heavy cavalry ultimately decided the issue. A large number of Indian soldiers lost their lives. Prithviraj escaped but was captured near Saraswati (Sirsa). The Turkish armies captured the fortresses of Hansi, Saraswati, and Samana, then attacked and captured Ajmer. Prithviraj was allowed to rule over Ajmer for some time, as coins from this period bear the legend “Prithvirajadeva” on one side and “Sri Muhammad Sam” on the other.

        Soon after, Prithviraj was executed on charges of “conspiracy,” and Prithviraj’s son succeeded him. Delhi was also restored to its Tomar ruler, but this policy was reversed soon after. The ruler of Delhi was ousted, and Delhi was made a base for further Turkish advance into the Ganga valley. Following a rebellion, a Muslim army recaptured Ajmer and installed a Turkish general there. Prithviraj’s son moved to Ranthambor and founded a new powerful Chauhan kingdom there.

        Thus, the Delhi area and eastern Rajasthan passed under Turkish rule.

    Turkish Conquest of the Ganga Valley
        Bihar and Bengal
            Between 1192 and 1206, Turkish rule extended over the Ganga-Jamuna Doab and its neighboring regions, including Bihar and Bengal. To establish control in the Doab, the Turks first had to defeat the powerful Gahadavala kingdom of Kanauj. The Gahadavala ruler, Jaichandra, had ruled peacefully for two decades but was not a very capable warrior, having earlier suffered a defeat at the hands of the Sena king of Bengal.

            After the Battle of Tarain, Muizzuddin returned to Ghazni, leaving Indian affairs in the hands of his trusted slave, Qutbuddin Aibak. Over the next two years, the Turks overran parts of the upper Doab without opposition from the Gahadavalas. In 1194, Muizzuddin returned to India, crossing the Yamuna with 50,000 cavalry to move towards Kanauj. A fiercely contested battle was fought at Chandawar near Kanauj, where Jaichandra was killed by an arrow, and his army was defeated. Muizzuddin then advanced to Banaras, destroying numerous temples. The Turks established control over a vast territory up to the borders of Bihar.

            Thus, the battles of Tarain and Chandawar laid the foundation for Turkish rule in northern India. However, consolidating this conquest took nearly fifty years.

            Muizzuddin lived until 1206. During his reign, he occupied the forts of Bayana and Gwalior to guard Delhi’s southern flank. Soon after, Aibak conquered Kalinjar, Mahoba, and Khajuraho from the Chandel rulers.

            With a base in the Doab, the Turks launched raids into neighboring areas. Aibak defeated Bhima III, the ruler of Gujarat, ravaging Anhilwara and other towns. However, the Turks were not strong enough to rule such far-flung areas.

            The Turks found more success in the east. Bakhtiyar Khalji, a Khalji officer, was appointed in charge of areas beyond Banaras. He frequently raided Bihar, which was then largely unprotected. During these raids, he destroyed famous Buddhist monasteries in Bihar, including Nalanda and Vikramasila, and accumulated wealth and followers. He also gathered information about routes to Bengal, a rich land known for its internal resources and flourishing trade.

            After careful preparations, Bakhtiyar Khalji marched towards Nadia, where the Sena ruler Lakshmana Sena had built a palace and gone on pilgrimage. Disguised as a horse-merchant, Bakhtiyar Khalji attacked the palace, creating confusion. Taken by surprise, Lakshmana Sena escaped, and Bakhtiyar’s forces soon overpowered the garrison. The wealth of Lakshmana, including his family, was captured. Bakhtiyar then occupied Lakhnauti, the Sena capital, without opposition. Lakshmana Sena retreated to Sonargaon in southern Bengal, where he continued to rule.

            Although Muizzuddin appointed Bakhtiyar Khalji as Bengal’s governor, Bakhtiyar ruled largely as an independent ruler. However, his reign was short-lived. After foolishly undertaking an expedition into the Brahmaputra valley, Bakhtiyar’s army faced defeat in Assam. The Turkish forces were exhausted by hunger and illness, and when they were trapped between a river and the Assamese army, they suffered a total defeat. Bakhtiyar barely escaped with a few followers, but his health was broken, and he died after being stabbed by one of his own officers.

            Meanwhile, Muizzuddin and his brother sought to expand the Ghurid empire into Central Asia. However, in 1203, Muizzuddin suffered a disastrous defeat against the Khwarizmi empire, leading him to focus solely on India. This allowed the rise of a Turkish state centered in India. After the defeat, the Khokhars, a tribe in western Punjab, revolted, and Muizzuddin led a large-scale campaign against them in 1206. He massacred the Khokhars, which silenced their rebellion. On his way back to Ghazni, Muizzuddin was killed by a Muslim fanatic belonging to a rival sect.

            Muizzuddin Muhammad bin Sam is often compared to Mahmud of Ghazni. While Mahmud had a larger empire and was more successful in Central Asia, Muizzuddin’s political achievements in India were greater. Mahmud’s conquest of Punjab paved the way for Muizzuddin’s successes in northern India. However, the conditions facing the two rulers were very different, making direct comparisons difficult. Despite using the slogan of Islam to justify their conquests and looting, neither ruler was truly motivated by religious zeal, as they allowed defeated rulers to retain their kingdoms unless it was necessary to annex them.

    CAUSES OF THE DEFEAT OF THE RAJPUTS
        The defeat of the leading states of north India within a short span of about 15 years by the Turkish armies requires some explanation. It can be stated as an axiom that a country is conquered by another only when it suffers from social and political weaknesses or becomes economically and militarily backward compared to its neighbors. Recent research shows that the Turks did not have superior weapons compared to the Indians. The iron stirrup, which had changed the mode of warfare in Europe, had spread in India from the 8th century onwards. The Turkish bows could shoot arrows over longer distances, but the Indian bows were considered more accurate and deadly, with arrowheads generally dipped in poison. In hand-to-hand combat, Indian swords were considered the best in the world. The Indians also had the advantage of elephants. Perhaps the Turks had horses that were swifter and more sturdy than the horses imported into India.

        The weaknesses of the Indians were social and organizational. The growth of feudalism, i.e., the rise of local landed elements and chiefs, had weakened the administrative structure and military organization of the Indian states. The rulers had to depend more on various chiefs who rarely acted in coordination and quickly dispersed to their areas after battle. On the other hand, the tribal structure of the Turks and the growth of the Iqta and Ahalisa systems enabled the Turks to maintain large standing armies that could be kept in the field for a long time. The Indians were not accustomed to moving as an organized body of horsemen that could cover long distances and fight and maneuver. Nor, it seems, did the Rajputs have large bodies of mounted archers or heavily armed cavalry. But for these factors, the Rajput states, many of which had greater human and physical resources at their disposal than the Ghaznavid and Ghurid empires, would not have suffered defeat or would have been able to recover if they had been defeated in battle.

        The social and organizational structure of the Turks gave them many advantages. The Iqta system, which grew slowly in West Asia, implied that a Turkish chief was allotted a piece of land as an Iqta from which he could collect the land revenues and taxes due to the state. In return, he had to maintain a body of troops for the service of the ruler. The grant was not hereditary and was held at the pleasure of the Sultan, who could transfer him to any place. The Sultan drew revenues directly from pieces of land known as Ahalisa. This enabled him to maintain a large standing army. While states rose and fell in both West Asia and India, in West Asia, unlike the Rajput states, the armed forces were more highly centralized in each state. Many of the Turkish officers were slaves who had been trained for warfare, grew in the Sultan’s service, and could be trusted by the Sultan.

        In terms of personal bravery, the Rajputs were in no way inferior to the Turks. In this context, the role of religion and caste in the military organization should not be unduly exaggerated. While the Turks were imbued with the ‘ghazi’ spirit, the Rajputs considered retreat in battle to be dishonorable. Caste did not prevent non-Rajputs or the Avarna (lower castes) from participating in battles, so Rajput armies were often larger in number than those fielded by the Turks.

        Finally, the Rajput defeat at the hands of Muizzuddin Muhammad should be viewed in the context of the past few centuries. From the end of the 10th century, the Turks had started infiltrating India. The Rajputs did put up spirited and prolonged resistance and defeated the Turkish armies several times. However, the Rajputs lacked what might be called ‘strategic vision’. Once the outer bastions of India—Kabul and Lahore—had fallen to the Turks, no concerted attempt was made by the Rajputs to recover them. Thus, little effort was made to push the Ghaznavids out of the Punjab. The Rajputs’ focus remained fixed on India, and they paid little attention to developments outside, especially in Central Asia, which had often played a key role in shaping the history of India.


- The Delhi Sultanate—I (Circa 1200-1400)
    I. The Mamluk Sultans (Thirteenth Century)
        Some of the factors that enabled the Turks to extend their conquest from the Punjab and Multan into the Ganga valley, and even to overrun Bihar and parts of Bengal, have been mentioned in the previous chapter.
