In this period, temples became very elaborate in structure and organization; even old temples were amplified by the addition of pillared halls, pavilions, and other subordinate structures. However, speaking of the broad toleration that prevailed in the empire of Krishna Deva, Barbosa says, “The king allows such freedom that every man may come and go and live according to his own creed, without suffering any annoyance, and without enquiry whether he is a Christian, Jew, Moor, or heathen.” Barbosa also pays tribute to Krishna Deva for the justice and equity prevailing in his empire.

        After the death of Krishna Deva (1530), there was a struggle for succession among his relatives, as his sons were all minors. Ultimately, in 1543, Sadashiva Raya ascended the throne and reigned until 1567. But the real power lay in the hands of a triumvirate, with the leading person being Rama Raja. Rama Raja was able to play off the various Muslim powers against one another. He entered into a commercial treaty with the Portuguese, stopping the supply of horses to the Bijapur ruler. In a series of wars, he completely defeated the Bijapur ruler and also inflicted humiliating defeats on Golconda and Ahmadnagar. It seems that Rama Raja had no larger purpose than to maintain a balance of power favourable to Vijayanagara between these three powers. Eventually, they combined to inflict a crushing defeat on Vijayanagara at Bannihatti, near Talikota, in 1565. This is also called the Battle of Talikota or the Battle of Rakshasa-Tangadi. Rama Raja was surrounded, taken prisoner, and immediately executed. It is said that 100,000 Hindus were slain during the battle. Vijayanagara was thoroughly looted and left in ruins.

        The Battle of Bannihatti is generally considered to mark the end of the great age of Vijayanagara. Although the kingdom lingered on for almost one hundred more years, its territories continuously decreased, and the Raya no longer played a significant role in the political affairs of South India.

    State and Economy under Vijayanagara
        The concept of kingship among the Vijayanagara rulers was elevated. In his book on polity, Krishna Deva Raya advises the king: “With great care and according to your power, you should attend to the work of protecting (the good) and punishing (the wicked) without neglecting anything that you see or hear.” He also enjoined the king to “levy taxes from his people moderately.”

        In the Vijayanagara kingdom, the king was advised by a council of ministers, which consisted of the great nobles of the kingdom. The kingdom was divided into rafyes or mandalam (provinces), below which were nadu (districts), shale (sub-districts), and granza (villages).

        The Chola traditions of village self-government were considerably weakened under Vijayanagara rule. The growth of hereditary Nayakships tended to curb their freedom and initiative. The governors of the provinces were royal princes at first. Later, persons belonging to vassal ruling families and nobles were also appointed as governors. The provincial governors had a large measure of autonomy. They held their own courts, appointed their own officers, and maintained their own armies. They were allowed to issue their own coins, though of small denominations only. There was no regular term for a provincial governor; his term depended on his ability and strength. The governor had the right to impose new taxes or remit old ones. Each governor paid a fixed contribution in men and money to the central government. It had been estimated that while the income of the kingdom was 12,000,000 paradas, the central government received only half the amount.

        There were many areas in the empire under the control of subordinate rulers—those who had been defeated in war but whose kingdoms had been restored to them. In the large centrally controlled area, the king granted ameram (territory with a fixed revenue) to military chiefs. These chiefs, called palatyagar (palegar) or Nayaks, had to maintain a fixed number of foot soldiers, horses, and elephants for the service of the state. The Nayaks or Palegars also had to pay a sum of money to the central exchequer. They formed a very powerful section, and sometimes it was difficult for the government to control them. These internal weaknesses of the Vijayanagara Empire contributed to its defeat in the Battle of Bannihatti and its subsequent disintegration. Many of the Nayaks, such as those of Tanjore and Madurai, became independent from that time.

        Historians are not agreed about the economic condition of the peasantry under Vijayanagara rule, because most travellers had little knowledge about village life and thus spoke of it in very general terms. In general, it may be presumed that the economic life of the people remained more or less the same. Their houses were mostly thatched with small doors. They generally went about barefooted and wore little above the waist. People of the upper classes sometimes wore costly shoes and a silk turban on their heads but did not cover themselves above the waist. All classes of people were fond of ornaments and wore them in their ears, on their necks, and on their arms.

        We have very little idea about the share of the produce the peasants were required to pay. According to an inscription, the rates of taxes were as follows:
            •	One-third of the produce of aruvai (a type of rice) during winter.
            •	One-fourth of sesame, ragi, horsegram, etc.
            •	One-sixth of millet and other crops cultivated on dry land.

        Thus, the rate varied according to the type of crops, soil, and method of irrigation.

        In addition to the land tax, there were various other taxes, such as property tax, tax on the sale of produce, profession taxes, military contributions (in times of distress), tax on marriage, etc. The sixteenth-century traveller, Nikitin, says, “The land is overstocked with people, but those in the country are very miserable while the nobles are extremely affluent and delight in luxury.”

        Urban life grew under the Vijayanagara Empire, and trade flourished. Towns grew, many of them around temples. The temples were very large and needed supplies of foodstuffs and commodities for the distribution of prasadam to the pilgrims, service of the god, the priests, etc. The temples were rich and also took an active part in trade, both internal and overseas. There was considerable growth of towns and urbanization under Vijayanagara rule. It is in this sense that many historians consider the period of Vijayanagara rule to be a period of transition from the old to the new economy.

    THE ADVENT OF THE PORTUGUESE
        The landing of Vasco da Gama at Calicut in 1498 with two ships, guided by Gujarati pilots who had navigated the ships from the African coast to Calicut, is often considered the beginning of a new phase in which European control over the oceans began. Indian trade and traders suffered a setback, and eventually, the Europeans were able to establish their colonial rule and domination over India and most neighboring countries. However, this view has been questioned by both Western and Indian historians, particularly after World War II and the end of European political rule in the region.

        Before we assess the impact of the Portuguese on Indian society, economy, and politics, it is important to examine the factors that brought the Portuguese to India. Broadly speaking, the Portuguese arrived at a time when the European economy was growing rapidly. This growth was facilitated by the expansion of land under cultivation due to the drainage of marshes, deforestation, the introduction of improved ploughs, and more scientific crop rotation methods. These factors led to an increase in cattle production and meat supply. The growth was reflected in the rise of towns and an increase in both internal and external trade. Since Roman times, there had been a steady demand for oriental goods, including silk from China and spices and drugs from India and Southeast Asia. With economic revival, this demand increased, particularly for pepper and spices, which were used to preserve meat, as many cattle were slaughtered during the winter due to fodder shortages. Pepper was brought to the Levant, Egypt, and the Black Sea areas overland and partly by sea from India and Southeast Asia.

        With the rise of the Ottoman Turks in the early 15th century, these regions came under Turkish control. The Turks captured Constantinople in 1453, followed by Syria and Egypt. While the Turks were not opposed to trade, their monopoly on pepper negatively impacted European traders. The expansion of Turkish power towards Eastern Europe and the growth of the Turkish navy, which made the eastern Mediterranean a Turkish-controlled area, also alarmed Europeans. Venice and Genoa, the most active traders in oriental goods, were too small to resist the Turks, and Venice, in particular, quickly came to terms with them. The banner of resistance against the Turkish threat was therefore taken up by Spain and Portugal, with assistance from North Europeans and the Genoese, rivals of Venice. It was not only the Portuguese, but also all these forces, who began the search for a direct sea route to India, sparking the era of naval discoveries, including the “discovery” of America by the Genoese navigator Christopher Columbus (or its rediscovery, since Norsemen had reached America earlier, along with the Red Indians across the Bering Straits). The work of the Portuguese ruler Dom Henrique, also known as Henry the Navigator, should be viewed in this context.

        From 1418, Prince Henry sent two or three ships every year to explore the western coast of Africa and search for a sea route to India. His objectives were twofold: first, to oust the Arabs and his European rivals, the Venetians, from the rich eastern trade, and second, to counterbalance the growing power of the Turks and Arabs by converting the “heathens” of Africa and Asia to Christianity. Both objectives were pursued steadily, with the Pope granting Portugal a “perpetual” right to any lands discovered beyond Cape Nor in Africa up to India, on the condition that the people of those lands be converted to Christianity.

        In 1488, Bartholomew Diaz rounded the Cape of Good Hope, paving the way for direct trade links between Europe and India. These long sea voyages were made possible by remarkable inventions, such as the mariner’s compass and the astrolabe, which allowed navigators to determine the height of celestial bodies. The compass was known to the Chinese centuries earlier, though not widely used, while the astrolabe was commonly used by Arabs, Indians, and others. While European ships were not superior to those used in Asia, such as the Chinese junks, the Europeans displayed a new spirit of daring and enterprise. This spirit was fueled by the revival of trade and commerce from the 13th century, leading to intense rivalry among European states. The Renaissance, a new intellectual movement, encouraged independent investigation, rather than relying on traditional religious teachings. These developments facilitated the rapid assimilation and improvement of foreign inventions like gunpowder, printing, and the telescope, as well as advancements in metallurgy, which helped produce better-quality guns.

        Vasco da Gama landed at Calicut in 1498, with Gujarati pilots on board. The strong colony of Arab merchants there was hostile, but the Zamorin welcomed the Portuguese and allowed them to take pepper, drugs, and other goods on board. In Portugal, the goods brought by Gama were valued at sixty times the cost of the entire expedition. Despite this, direct trade between India and Europe grew slowly. One reason for this was the monopoly exercised by the Portuguese government. From the outset, the Portuguese rulers were determined to treat the eastern trade as a royal monopoly, excluding not only rival nations in Europe and Asia, but also private Portuguese traders.

        Alarmed by the growing power of the Portuguese, the Sultan of Egypt sent a fleet towards India. This fleet was joined by ships from the ruler of Gujarat. After an initial victory in which the Portuguese governor’s son, Don Almeida, was killed, the combined fleet was defeated by the Portuguese in 1509. This victory made the Portuguese navy supreme in the Indian Ocean, allowing them to extend their operations to the Persian Gulf and the Red Sea.

        Shortly after, Albuquerque succeeded as the governor of Portuguese possessions in the East. He embarked on a policy of establishing forts at strategic locations in Asia and Africa, to be supported by a strong navy. He argued that a dominion founded solely on a navy could not last, and that fortifications were essential for trade and friendly relations.

        Albuquerque’s new policy began with the capture of Goa from Bijapur in 1510. Goa’s excellent harbor and strategic location made it the ideal base for Portuguese commercial and political activities in the East. From Goa, the Portuguese were able to extend their influence to the Gujarat sea-ports, blockading and sacking Bijapur’s sea trade.

        From Goa, the Portuguese further strengthened their position by establishing forts at Colombo in Sri Lanka, Achin in Sumatra, and at the Malacca port, which controlled the narrow gulf between the Malay Peninsula and Sumatra. They also established a station at the island of Socotra at the mouth of the Red Sea. Although they failed to capture Aden, they forced the ruler of Ormuz, who controlled the Persian Gulf entrance, to allow them to establish a fort there.

        However, the success of the Portuguese was more apparent than real. They faced external challenges from the Turks, who were sometimes joined by Arabs and Indian powers. After conquering Syria, Egypt, and Arabia, the Turks moved into Eastern Europe and, in 1529, threatened Vienna. The growth of Turkish power in the Red Sea and Persian Gulf seemed to signal a conflict between the Turks and Portuguese for control over the western part of the Indian Ocean.

        In response to the growing Portuguese threat to Gujarat’s trade and coastal areas, the Sultan of Gujarat sent an embassy to the Ottoman ruler, congratulating him on his victories and seeking support. The Ottomans, in turn, expressed a desire to combat the Portuguese. From this point onward, continuous exchanges of embassies and letters occurred between the two powers. The Turks ousted the Portuguese from the Red Sea, and in 1529 a strong fleet under Sulaiman Rais was dispatched to aid Bahadur Shah, the ruler of Gujarat.

        In 1531, after intrigues with local officials, the Portuguese attacked Daman and Diu, but the Ottoman commander, Rumi Khan, repulsed the attack. The Portuguese, however, built a fort at Chaul, further down the coast.

        Before the Gujarat-Turkish alliance could be solidified, the Mughals presented a greater threat to Gujarat. Humayun attacked Gujarat, and in response, Bahadur Shah granted the Portuguese the island of Bassein. A defensive-offensive alliance against the Mughals was also established, allowing the Portuguese to build a fort at Diu. This allowed the Portuguese to establish a foothold in Gujarat.

        Soon after, Bahadur Shah regretted his concessions to the Portuguese. Following the expulsion of the Mughals from Gujarat, he again appealed to the Ottoman Sultan for help and sought to limit Portuguese encroachments at Diu. During negotiations, Bahadur Shah, aboard one of the governor’s ships, suspected treachery. A scuffle ensued, during which the Portuguese governor was killed and Bahadur Shah drowned while swimming ashore in 1536.

        Although the Ottoman sultans claimed to be champions of Islam and opponents of the Portuguese, they did not seriously contest Portuguese control in the Persian Gulf and beyond. The Turkish navy dominated the eastern Mediterranean, and they made raids beyond Gibraltar.

        The Turks made their most significant naval attempt against the Portuguese in Indian waters in 1536. Their fleet consisted of 45 galleons with 20,000 men, including 7,000 land soldiers. The fleet, led by Sulaiman Pasha, a trusted Ottoman official, besieged Diu in 1538. However, the Turkish admiral’s arrogance led the Sultan of Gujarat to withdraw his support, and after two months, the fleet retired following news of a formidable Portuguese armada.

        The Turkish threat to the Portuguese persisted for another two decades. In 1551, Peri Rais, assisted by the Zamorin of Calicut, attacked the Portuguese forts at Muscat and Ormuz. Meanwhile, the Portuguese strengthened their position by securing Daman from its ruler. A final Ottoman expedition was sent under Ali Rais in 1554. The failure of these expeditions resulted in a final change in the Turkish attitude. In 1566, the Portuguese and the Ottomans came to an agreement to share the spice and Indian trade and avoid conflict in the Arabian Sea. The Ottomans shifted their interest once again to Europe and came to an agreement with the Portuguese to divide the oriental trade between them. This precluded their alliance with the rising Mughal power against the Portuguese. However, it also had economic consequences, as we shall see.

    Portuguese Impact on the Indian Trade, Society, and Politics
        From the beginning, the Portuguese could neither adequately police the vast expanse of the Indian Ocean nor control the trade or the traders there. All they could attempt to do was monopolize certain goods and tax others. Thus, trade in pepper, arms and ammunition, and war horses was declared a royal monopoly. No nation, not even Portuguese private traders, were allowed to engage in the trade of these goods. Ships engaged in the trade of other commodities had to take a permit from the Portuguese officials. The Portuguese also attempted to force all ships going to the East or to Africa to pass by Goa and pay customs duty there.

        To enforce these rules, the Portuguese arrogated to themselves the right to search any ship suspected of engaging in ‘contraband’ trade. Ships that refused to be searched could be treated as prizes of war and either sunk or captured, with the men and women aboard treated as slaves. This led to continuous friction. The Portuguese soon found that they stood to lose more on land than they gained at sea by such practices, which were quite contrary to the practices and traditions of open trade in Asian waters. True, many Arab and Indian ships began to carry cannons and soldiers, but it was more a safeguard against sea pirates that infested the Malabar and Arab coasts.

        The Portuguese were hardly able to change the established pattern of Asian trade networks. The Gujarati and Arab traders continued to dominate some of the most lucrative Asian trade, such as the trade in Indian textiles, supplemented by rice and sugar, in exchange for spices from the Southeast, gold and horses from West Asia, and silk and porcelain from China. The Portuguese were not even able to monopolize the pepper and spice trade to Europe, except for a couple of decades at the outset. By the end of the sixteenth century, the supply of pepper to the Levant and Egyptian markets, both overland and by the Red Sea, was as large as it had been earlier. This was because the great Asian empires, the Mughal and the Safavid, were able to promote and safeguard overland trade, and also because the Gujaratis were able to arrange a new supply route from Achin in Sumatra to Egypt via Lakshadweep and the Red Sea, where the Portuguese navy could not operate.

        Nor were the Portuguese able to develop Goa as the dominant center of the Asian trade, eclipsing Cambay and later Surat in Gujarat. However, they adversely affected the Malabar trade and the sea trade from Bengal, which they preyed upon from Chittagong.

        The Portuguese, however, opened up India’s trade with Japan, from which copper and silver were obtained. They also opened up India’s trade with the Philippines. From the Philippines, the Portuguese carried Indian textiles to South America and brought back silver in exchange. They demonstrated how naval power could be used to harass and hamper the trade of even such a well-developed country as India, as well as to open new lines of trade.

        The Portuguese could not act as a bridge for transmitting to India the science and technology that had grown in Europe since the Renaissance. This was partly because the Portuguese were themselves not as deeply affected by the Renaissance as Italy and Northern Europe. Later, with the growth of a Catholic religious reaction led by the Jesuits, they even set their face against the new science. However, they did help transmit a number of agricultural products from Central America, such as potatoes, tobacco, maize, peanuts, etc. These became widespread only gradually.

        The defeat of Vijayanagara at Banihatti in 1565 emboldened the Deccani states to make a concerted effort to dislodge the Portuguese from the Deccan coast. As long as Vijayanagara had threatened Bijapur in the south, peace with the Portuguese was essential since they controlled the horse trade, and hostilities with them would have meant a diversion of the trade in favor of Vijayanagara. In 1570, Ali Adil Shah, the Sultan of Bijapur, entered into an agreement with the Sultan of Ahmadnagar. The Zamorin of Calicut was also drawn into the alliance. The allies decided to attack the Portuguese positions in their own dominions. Adil Shah personally led the attack against Goa, while Nizam Shah besieged Chaul. But once again, the Portuguese defense, backed up by their navy, proved to be too strong. Thus, the Portuguese remained masters of the Indian seas and the Deccan coast.


- Struggle for Empire in North India—I (Circa 1400-1525)
    The growing weakness of the Delhi Sultanate, and the attack of Timur on Delhi in 1398, followed by the flight of the Tughlaq king from his capital, emboldened a number of provincial governors and autonomous rulers to declare their independence. Apart from the Deccan states, Bengal in the east, and Sindh and Multan in the west were among the first to break away from Delhi. Soon, the governors of Gujarat, Malwa, and Jaunpur (in eastern Uttar Pradesh) also declared themselves independent. With the expulsion of the Muslim governor from Ajmer, the various states of Rajputana asserted their independence.

    Gradually, a definite pattern of balance of power emerged between the states belonging to the various regions. In the west, Gujarat, Malwa, and Mewar balanced and checked the growth of each other’s power. Bengal was checked by the Gajapati rulers of Orissa, as well as by Jaunpur. The rise of the power of the Lodis in Delhi from about the middle of the fifteenth century resulted in a long-drawn-out tussle between them and the rulers of Jaunpur for the mastery of the Ganga-Yamuna valley. The situation began to change with the absorption of Jaunpur by the Lodis towards the end of the fifteenth century. Following this victory, the Lodis began to expand their power in eastern Rajasthan and Malwa. Malwa started disintegrating at this time due to internal factors, thereby sharpening the rivalry between Gujarat, Mewar, and the Lodis. It appeared that the victor in this contest would dominate the entire north India. Thus, the struggle for the domination of Malwa was the cockpit for the struggle for the mastery of north India. It was this heightened rivalry which perhaps prompted Rana Sanga to invite Babur in the hope that the destruction of the power of the Lodis would leave Mewar as the strongest power in the field.

    Eastern India—Bengal, Assam, and Orissa
        As we have seen above, Bengal had frequently become independent of the control of Delhi due to its distance, climate, and the fact that much of its communication depended upon waterways, with which the Turkish rulers were unfamiliar. Due to the preoccupation of Muhammad Tughlaq with rebellions in various quarters, Bengal again broke away from Delhi in 1338. Four years later, one of the nobles, Ilyas Khan, captured Lakhnauti and Sonargaon, and ascended the throne under the title Sultan Shamsuddin Ilyas Khan. He extended his dominions in the west, from Tirhut to Champaran and Gorakhpur, and finally up to Banaras. This forced Firuz Tughlaq to undertake a campaign against him. Marching through Champaran and Gorakhpur, the territories newly acquired by Ilyas, Firuz Tughlaq occupied the Bengali capital Pandua and forced Ilyas to seek shelter in the strong fort of Ekdala. After a siege of two months, Firuz tempted Ilyas out of the fort by feigning flight. The Bengali forces were defeated, but Ilyas once again retreated into Ekdala. Finally, a treaty of friendship was concluded by which the river Kosi in Bihar was fixed as the boundary between the two kingdoms. Though Ilyas exchanged regular gifts with Firuz, he was in no way subordinate to him. Friendly relations with Delhi enabled Ilyas to extend his control over the kingdom of Kamrup (in modern Assam).

        Ilyas Shah was a popular ruler and had many achievements to his credit. When Firuz was at Pandua, he tried to win over the inhabitants of the city to his side by giving liberal grants of land to the nobles, the clergy, and other deserving people. His attempts failed. The popularity of Ilyas might have been one of the reasons for the failure of Firuz against him.

        Firuz Tughlaq invaded Bengal a second time when Ilyas died and his son, Sikandar, succeeded to the throne. Sikandar followed the tactics of his father and retreated to Ekdala. Firuz failed, once again, to capture it, and had to beat a retreat. After this, Bengal was left alone for about 200 years and was not invaded again until after the Mughals had established their power at Delhi. It was overrun by Sher Shah in 1538. During this period, a number of dynasties flourished in Bengal. The frequent changes of dynasties did not, however, disturb the even tenor of the lives of the common people.

        The most famous sultan in the dynasty of Ilyas Shah was Ghiyasuddin Azam Shah (1389-1409). He was known for his love of justice. It is said that he once accidentally killed the son of a widow who complained to the qazī. The sultan, when summoned to the court, humbly appeared and paid the fine imposed by the qazī. At the end of the trial, the sultan told the qazī that if he had failed to do his duty, he would have had him beheaded.

        Azam Shah had close relations with the famous learned men of his times, including the famous Persian poet, Hafiz of Shiraz. He re-established friendly relations with the Chinese. The Chinese emperor received his envoy cordially and, in 1409, sent his own envoy with presents to the sultan and his wife, along with a request to send Buddhist monks to China. This was accordingly done. Incidentally, this shows that Buddhism had not died completely in Bengal until then.

        The revival of contact with China helped in the growth of the overseas trade of Bengal. The Chittagong port became a flourishing port for trade with China and for the re-export of Chinese goods to other parts of the world.

        During this period, there was a brief spell of Hindu rule under Raja Ganesh. However, his sons preferred to rule as Muslims.

        The sultans of Bengal adorned their capitals, Pandua and Gaur, with magnificent buildings. These had a style of their own, distinct from the style which had developed in Delhi. The materials used were both stone and brick. The sultans also patronized the Bengali language. The celebrated poet Maladhar Basu, compiler of Sn-Krishna-Vijaya, was patronized by the sultans and was granted the title of Gunaraja Khan. His son was honored with the title of Satyaraja Khan. But the most significant period for the growth of the Bengali language was the rule of Alauddin Hussain (1493-1519). Some of the famous Bengali writers of the time flourished under his rule.

        A brilliant period began under the enlightened rule of Alauddin Hussain. The sultan restored law and order and adopted a liberal policy by offering high offices to the Hindus. Thus, his wazir was a talented Hindu. The chief physician, the chief of the bodyguard, and the master of the mint were also Hindus. The two famous brothers who were celebrated as pious Vaishnavas, Rupa and Sanatan, held high posts, one of them being the sultan’s private secretary. The sultan is also said to have shown great respect to the famous Vaishnavite saint, Chaitanya.

        Since the time of Muhammad bin Bakhtiyar Khalji, the Muslim rulers of Bengal had tried to bring the Brahmaputra valley in modern Assam under their control, but had to suffer a series of disastrous defeats in this region, which was little known to them. The independent sultans of Bengal tried to follow in the footsteps of their predecessors. There were two warring kingdoms in north Bengal and Assam at that time. Kamata (called Kamrup by the writers of the time) was in the west, and the Ahom kingdom was in the east. The Ahoms, a Mongoloid tribe from north Burma, had succeeded in establishing a powerful kingdom in the thirteenth century, and had become Hinduized in the course of time. The name Assam is derived from them.

        Ilyas Shah invaded Kamta and, it seems, penetrated up to Gauliati. However, he could not hold the area, and the river Karatoya was accepted as the northeast boundary of Bengal. Plundering raids into Kamta by some of the successors of Ilyas Shah did not change the situation. The rulers of Kamta were gradually able to recover many of the areas on the eastern bank of the Karatoya. They also fought against the Ahoms. By alienating both their neighbors, they sealed their doom. An attack by Alauddin Hussain Shah, which was supported by the Ahoms, led to the destruction of the city of Kamtapur (in modern Cooch Bihar), and the annexation of the kingdom to Bengal. The sultan appointed one of his sons as governor of the area. A colony of Afghans was planted in the area. A subsequent attack on the Ahom kingdom, probably by Nusrat Shah, the son of Alauddin Hussain, was unsuccessful and was repulsed with considerable losses. The eastern Brahmaputra valley was at this time under Suhungmung, who is considered the greatest of the Ahom rulers. He changed his name to Svarga Narayana. This was an index of the rapid Hinduization of the Ahoms. He not only repulsed the Muslim attack, but also extended his kingdom in all directions. The Vaishnavite reformer, Shankaradeva, belonged to his time and played an important role in the spread of Vaishnavism in the area.

        The sultans of Bengal also tried to bring Chittagong and a part of Arakan under their control. Sultan Hussain Shah not only wrested Chittagong from the Arakan king but also conquered Tipperah from its ruler.

        The rulers of Bengal had also to contend with Orissa. During the period of the Sultanate rule over Bengal, the Ganga rulers of Orissa had aided Radha (south Bengal), and even made an attempt at the conquest of Lakhnauti. These attacks had been repulsed, but the rulers of Orissa were powerful enough not to allow the governor of Bengal to penetrate into Orissa. It was the rulers of the Ganga dynasty who built the famous Puri temple, and the Sun temple (Konark). It was only after 1338 that the independent ruler of Bengal, Ilyas Shah, raided Jajnagar (Orissa). It is said that overcoming all opposition, he advanced up to the Chilka Lake and returned with a rich booty, including a number of elephants. A couple of years later, in 1360, while returning from his Bengal campaign, Firuz Tughlaq also raided Orissa. He occupied the capital city, massacred a large number of people, and desecrated the Jagannatha temple of Puri. These two raids destroyed the prestige of the Ganga dynasty. In due course, a new dynasty, called the Gajapati dynasty, came to the fore. The Gajapati rule marks a brilliant phase in Orissa’s history. The rulers were great builders and warriors. The Gajapati rulers were mainly instrumental in extending their rule in the south towards Karnataka. As we have seen, this brought them into conflict with Vijayanagara, the Reddis, and the Bahmani sultans. Perhaps, one reason why the Gajapati rulers preferred aggrandizement in the south was their feeling that the sultans of Bengal were too strong to be easily dislodged from the Bengal-Orissa border. But the Orissa rulers could not hold on to their southern conquests for any length of time due to the power and capabilities of the Vijayanagara and Bahmani rulers.

        In Bengal, the Orissa frontier at this time was the river Saraswati, which then carried much of the waters of the Ganga. Thus, a large part of Midnapore district and part of the Hugli district were included in Orissa territories. There is some evidence that the Orissa rulers tried to extend their control up to Bhagirathi but were compelled to retreat. Some of the sultans of Bengal, including Alauddin Hussain Shah, made raids into Orissa, which extended up to Puri and Cuttack.

        Intermittent fighting also went on at the frontier. However, the rulers of Bengal were not able to dislodge the Orissan rulers from their frontiers, or to gain any territory beyond the river Saraswati. That the Orissan rulers were able to engage successfully in battles at the same time in such far-flung areas as Bengal and Karnataka testifies to their strength and prowess.

    WESTERN INDIA—GUJARAT, Malwa, AND Mewar
        On account of the excellence of its handicrafts, flourishing seaports, and the richness of its soil, Gujarat was one of the wealthiest provinces of the Delhi Sultanate. Under Firuz Tughlaq, Gujarat had a benevolent governor who, according to Ferishta, “encouraged the Hindu religion and thus promoted rather than suppressed the worship of idols.” He was succeeded by Zafar Khan, whose father, Sadharan, was a Rajput who had converted to Islam and had given his sister in marriage to Firuz Tughlaq. After Timur’s invasion of Delhi, both Gujarat and Malwa became independent in all but name. However, it was not until 1407 that Zafar Khan formally proclaimed himself ruler, adopting the title Muzaffar Shah.

        The real founder of the kingdom of Gujarat was Ahmad Shah I (1411–43), the grandson of Muzaffar Shah. During his long reign, he brought the nobility under control, settled the administration, and expanded and consolidated the kingdom. He moved the capital from Patan to the newly founded city of Ahmedabad, which he established in 1413. Ahmad Shah was a great builder, enhancing the city with magnificent palaces, bazaars, mosques, and madrasas. He drew on the rich architectural traditions of the Jains in Gujarat, creating a style distinctly different from that of Delhi. Some of its features include slender turrets, exquisite stone-carving, and highly ornate brackets. The Jama Masjid in Ahmedabad and the Tin Darwaza are fine examples of the architectural style during his reign.

        Ahmad Shah attempted to extend his control over the Rajput states in the Saurashtra region and those located along the Gujarat-Rajasthan border. In Saurashtra, he defeated and captured the strong fort of Girnar but restored it to the raja on the condition that he pay tribute. He then attacked Sidhpur, the famous Hindu pilgrimage center, and leveled many of its beautiful temples. Ahmad Shah imposed the jizyah tax on the Hindus in Gujarat, a tax that had not been levied on them earlier. While many medieval historians hailed Ahmad Shah as a great enemy of the infidels, modern historians often regard him as a bigot. However, the truth seems more complex. While Ahmad Shah acted as a bigot in ordering the destruction of Hindu temples, he did not hesitate to appoint Hindus in his government. Manik Chand and Motichand, from the Bania or trader community, served as ministers under him. Ahmad Shah was so strict in justice that he had his own son-in-law executed in the marketplace for a murder he had committed. Although he fought the Hindu rulers, he also fought against Muslim rulers, particularly those of Malwa. He subordinated the powerful fort of Idar and brought the Rajput states of Jhalawar, Bundi, and Dungarpur under his control.

        From the beginning, Gujarat and Malwa were bitter rivals and often found themselves on opposite sides of conflicts. Muzaffar Shah had defeated and imprisoned Hushang Shah, the ruler of Malwa. However, after struggling to control Malwa, he eventually released Hushang Shah after a few years and reinstated him. This did not heal the rift; instead, it made the rulers of Malwa even more wary of Gujarat’s power. They constantly sought to weaken Gujarat by supporting disaffected elements, such as rebel nobles or Hindu rajas at war with the Gujarat ruler. The rulers of Gujarat tried to counter this by attempting to install their own nominee on the throne of Malwa. This bitter rivalry weakened both kingdoms and made it impossible for them to play a larger role in the politics of north India.

        Mahmud Begarha
            The successors of Ahmad Shah continued his policy of expansion and consolidation. The most famous sultan of Gujarat was Mahmud Begarha, who ruled from 1459 to 1511. He was called Begarha because he captured two of the most powerful forts: Girnar in Saurashtra (now called Junagadh) and Champaner in southern Gujarat. The ruler of Girnar had been paying tribute regularly, but Mahmud Begarha decided to annex the kingdom as part of his policy to bring Saurashtra fully under control. Saurashtra was a rich and prosperous region with fertile tracts and flourishing ports. Unfortunately, it was also plagued by robbers and sea pirates who targeted trade and shipping. The powerful fort of Girnar was considered suitable not only for administering Saurashtra but also as a base for operations against Sindh.

            Mahmud Begarha besieged Girnar with a large force. Though the raja had only a few guns in the fort, he resisted gallantly, but to no avail. It is said that the conquest of this seemingly inaccessible fort was due to treason. The ruler of Girnar had forcibly taken the wife of his kamdar (minister), who secretly schemed for his downfall. After the fort fell, the raja embraced Islam and entered the service of the sultan. Mahmud Begarha founded a new town at the foot of the hill called Mustafabad. He built many lofty buildings there and instructed his nobles to do the same, making it the second capital of Gujarat.

            Later in his reign, Mahmud sacked Dwarka, claiming it harbored pirates who preyed on pilgrim traffic to Mecca. However, the campaign also served to destroy the famous Hindu temples there.

            The fort of Champaner was strategically located for Mahmud’s plans to bring Khandesh and Malwa under his control. The ruler, though a feudatory of Gujarat, had close relations with the sultan of Malwa. Champaner fell in 1454 after the valiant raja and his followers, deprived of help, performed the jausar ceremony and fought to the last man. Mahmud Begarha constructed a new town near Champaner, called Muhammadabad. He laid out many fine gardens and made it his principal residence.

            Champaner is now in ruins, but the Jama Masjid still stands, attracting attention. It has a covered courtyard, and many Jain architectural principles are incorporated. The stonework in the other buildings constructed during this period is so fine that it can only be compared to the work of goldsmiths.

            Mahmud Begarha also had to deal with the Portuguese, who were interfering with Gujarat’s trade with West Asia. He joined forces with the ruler of Egypt to counter Portuguese naval power, but was unsuccessful.

            During Mahmud Begarha’s long and peaceful reign, trade and commerce prospered. He constructed many caravanserais and inns for the comfort of travelers. Merchants were pleased because the roads were safe for traffic.

            Though Mahmud Begarha never received formal education, he gained considerable knowledge by associating with learned men. Many works were translated from Arabic into Persian during his reign. His court poet was Udayaraja, who composed in Sanskrit.

            Mahmud Begarha had a striking appearance. He had a flowing beard that reached his waist, and his moustache was so long that he tied it over his head. According to a traveler, Barbosa, Mahmud had been nourished on poison since childhood, so if a fly settled on his hand, it would swell and immediately die.

            Mahmud was also famous for his voracious appetite. It is said that for breakfast, he consumed a cup of honey, a cup of butter, and 100 to 150 plantains. He ate 10 to 15 kilos of food a day, and plates of meat patties (samosas) were placed on both sides of his pillow at night in case he became hungry.

            Under Mahmud Begarha, the Gujarat kingdom reached its maximum size and became one of the most powerful and well-administered states in the country. Later, it was powerful enough to pose a serious challenge to the Mughal ruler, Humayun.

        Malwa
            The state of Malwa was situated on the high plateau between the Narmada and Tapti rivers. It controlled the trunk routes between Gujarat and northern India, as well as those between north and south India.

            As long as Malwa remained strong, it acted as a barrier to the ambitions of Gujarat, Mewar, the Bahmanis, and the Lodi sultans of Delhi. The geopolitical situation in northern India was such that if any of the powerful states in the region could extend its control over Malwa, it would be well on its way to dominating the entire northern part of India.

            During the fifteenth century, the kingdom of Malwa reached the height of its glory. The capital was shifted from Dhar to Mandu, a location that was highly defensible and possessed natural beauty. Here, the rulers of Malwa constructed a large number of buildings, the ruins of which are still impressive today. Unlike the Gujarat style of architecture, Mandu architecture was massive, made even more so by using a lofty plinth for the buildings. The large-scale use of colored and glazed tiles provided variety to the structures. The best-known among them are the Jama Masjid, Hindola Mahal, and Jahaz Mahal.

            From the beginning, the kingdom of Malwa was torn by internal dissensions. The struggle for succession between different contenders for the throne was accompanied by fighting between various groups of nobles for power and profit. The neighboring states of Gujarat and Mewar were always ready to take advantage of this factionalism for their own benefit.

            One of the early rulers of Malwa, Hushang Shah, adopted a broad policy of religious toleration. Many Rajputs were encouraged to settle in Malwa. Two of the elder brothers of Rana Mokal of Mewar were granted jagirs in Malwa. The inscription of the Lalitpur temple, built during this period, suggests that no restrictions were placed on the construction of temples. Hushang Shah also extended his patronage to the Jains, who were the principal merchants and bankers of the area. Nardeva Soni, a successful merchant, became the treasurer and one of Hushang Shah’s advisers.

            However, not all rulers of Malwa were equally tolerant. Mahmud Khalji (1436–69), considered the most powerful of the Malwa rulers, destroyed many temples during his struggles with Rana Kumbha of Mewar and the neighboring Hindu rajas. While this was reprehensible and a setback from the policy of broad toleration that had gradually developed under the Delhi Sultanate, it was not part of a general policy of temple destruction.

            Mahmud Khalji was a restless and ambitious monarch. He fought with almost all his neighbors—the ruler of Gujarat, the rajas of Gondwana and Orissa, the Bahmani sultans, and even the sultan of Delhi. However, his principal efforts were focused on overrunning South Rajputana and subduing Mewar.

        Mewar
            The rise of Mewar during the fifteenth century was an important factor in the political life of northern India. After being ousted from Ajmer, the Chauhans shifted to Ranthambhor and established a powerful state. With the conquest of Ranthambhor by Alauddin Khalji, the power of the Chauhans in Rajputana finally came to an end. From its ruins, a number of new states emerged. The state of Marwar, with its capital at Jodhpur (founded in 1465), was one of these. Another important state in the region was the Muslim principality of Nagaur. Ajmer, which had been the seat of power for Muslim governors, changed hands several times and became a point of contention among the rising Rajput states. The mastery of eastern Rajputana was also disputed, with the ruler of Delhi being deeply interested in the region.

            The early history of the state of Mewar is obscure. The virtual founder of Mewar is said to be Bapa Rawal of the Guhilot clan, who migrated from Gujarat in the seventh century and dominated the southwestern part of Rajasthan. The Guhilots ruled from Chittor until the time of Ratan Singh, who was ousted by Alauddin Khalji. Chittor then passed into the hands of the Sisodiyas, who had been feudatories of the Guhilots. During the late fourteenth and early fifteenth centuries, Rao Lakha and Mokal made Mewar the most powerful state in Rajasthan. The ruler who elevated it to the status of a dominant power was Rana Kumbha (1433–68). After consolidating his position by defeating internal rivals, Kumbha focused on the conquest of Bundi, Kotah, and Daungarpur on the Gujarat border. Kotah had previously been loyal to Malwa, and Daungarpur to Gujarat. This brought him into direct conflict with both these kingdoms. There were also other reasons for the conflict. The Khan of Nagaur, whom Rana Kumbha had attacked, appealed for help to the ruler of Gujarat. Additionally, Rana Kumbha had sheltered a rival of Mahmud Khalji and even attempted to install him on the Malwa throne. In retaliation, Mahmud Khalji gave refuge to some of the Rana’s rivals, such as his brother Mokal.

            The conflicts with Gujarat and Malwa occupied Kumbha throughout his reign. He also had to contend with the Rathors of Marwar. Marwar, which had been under Mewar’s occupation, soon gained independence under the leadership of Rao Jodha.

            Although severely pressed from all sides, Rana Kumbha was largely able to maintain his position in Mewar. Kumbhalgarh was besieged a couple of times by Gujarat forces, while Mahmud Khalji managed to raid as far as Ajmer and install his own governor there. Nevertheless, the Rana was able to repel these attacks and retain most of his conquests, except for some outlying areas like Ranthambhor. Rana Kumbha’s ability to defend against two powerful states was a significant achievement.

            Kumbha was a patron of learned men and was himself a scholar. He composed several books, some of which are still extant. The ruins of his palace and the Victory Tower (Kirtistambha) at Chittor, which he built, show that he was also an enthusiastic builder. He dug several lakes and reservoirs for irrigation, and the temples built during his reign demonstrate that the art of stone-cutting and sculpture was still at a high level.

            Kumbha was murdered by his son, Uda, in a bid to gain the throne. Though Uda was soon ousted, he left behind a legacy of conflict. After a long fratricidal struggle, Rana Sanga, a grandson of Kumbha, ascended the throne of Mewar in 1508. The most important development between Kumbha’s death and the rise of Sanga was the rapid internal disintegration of Malwa. The ruler, Mahmud II, had fallen out with Medini Rai, the powerful Rajput leader of eastern Malwa who had helped him gain the throne. Mahmud II appealed to Gujarat for help, while Medini Rai sought assistance from Rana Sanga. In a battle in 1517, Rana Sanga defeated Mahmud II and took him prisoner to Chittor. It is said that he released him after six months, keeping one of Mahmud’s sons as a hostage. Eastern Malwa, including Chanderi, came under Rana Sanga’s control.

            The developments in Malwa alarmed the Lodi rulers of Delhi, who were closely monitoring the situation. Ibrahim Lodi of Delhi invaded Mewar but suffered a defeat at the hands of Rana Sanga at Khatoli. Ibrahim Lodi withdrew to consolidate his internal position. Meanwhile, Babur was approaching the gates of India.

            By 1525, the political situation in northern India was rapidly changing, and a decisive conflict for supremacy seemed inevitable.

    Northwest and North India – The Sharqis, The Lost Sultans, and Kashmir
        As we have seen, after the invasion of Timur, Sultan Mahmud Tughlaq fled from Delhi and took shelter first in Gujarat and then in Malwa. By the time he decided to return, the prestige of the throne of Delhi had been shattered. In the neighbourhood of Delhi itself, ambitious nobles and zamindars asserted their independence.

        Among the first to assert independence in the Ganga valley was Malik Sarwar, a prominent noble during the reign of Firuz Tughlaq. Malik Sarwar had served as the wazir for some time and was later nominated to the eastern areas with the title Matik-ws-Sharq (Lord of the East). His successors came to be called the Sharqis after his title. The Sharqi sultans fixed their capital at Jaunpur (in eastern Uttar Pradesh), which they beautified with magnificent palaces, mosques, and mausoleums. Only a few of these mosques and mausoleums survive today. They show that the Sharqi sultans did not merely copy the Delhi style of architecture but created a magnificent style of their own, marked by lofty gates and huge arches.

        The Sharqi sultans were great patrons of learning and culture. Poets, men of letters, scholars, and saints assembled at Jaunpur and shed lustre on it. In time, Jaunpur came to be known as the “Shiraz of the East.” Malik Muhammad Jaisi, the author of the well-known Hindi work Padmavat, lived at Jaunpur.

        The Sharqi Sultanate lasted for less than a century. At its height, it extended from Aligarh in western Uttar Pradesh to Darbhanga in north Bihar, and from the boundary of Nepal in the north to Bundelkhand in the south. The Sharqi rulers were eager to conquer Delhi, but they were not successful. With the establishment of the Lodis in Delhi towards the middle of the fifteenth century, the Sharqi rulers were gradually put on the defensive. They lost most of the areas in western Uttar Pradesh and exhausted themselves in a series of bitter but futile assaults on Delhi. At length, in 1484, Bahlul Lodi, the ruler of Delhi, occupied Jaunpur and annexed the Sharqi kingdom. The Sharqi king lived as an exile in Chunar for some time and died broken-hearted after repeated failures in regaining his kingdom.

        The Sharqi rulers maintained law and order over a large tract following the collapse of the government in Delhi. They successfully prevented the rulers of Bengal from extending their control over eastern Uttar Pradesh. Above all, they established a cultural tradition that continued long after the downfall of the Sharqis.

        After the Timurid invasion, a new dynasty called the Saiyid dynasty arose in Delhi. A number of Afghan sardars established themselves in the Punjab. The most important of these was Bahlul Lodi, who had been granted the iqta of Sirhind. Bahlul Lodi checked the growing power of the Khokhars, a fierce, warlike tribe living in the Salt Ranges. Soon, he dominated the entire Punjab. Called in to help the ruler of Delhi against an impending attack by the ruler of Mahwa, Bahlul stayed on. Before long, his men took control of Delhi. Bahlul formally crowned himself in 1451 when the ruler of Delhi died in exile, thus ending the Saiyid dynasty.

        The Lodis dominated the upper Ganga valley and the Punjab from the middle of the fifteenth century. Unlike the earlier Delhi rulers who were “Turks,” the Lodis were Afghans. Although Afghans formed a large group in the army of the Delhi Sultanate, very few Afghan nobles had been accorded important positions. The growing importance of the Afghans in north India was shown by the rise of Afghan rule in Malwa. In the south, they held important positions in the Bahmani kingdom.

        Bahlul Lodi’s energies were mainly occupied with his contest against the Sharqi rulers. Finding himself in a weak position, Bahlul invited the Afghans of Roh to come to India so that “they will get rid of the ignominy of poverty and I shall gain ascendancy.” The Afghan historian Abbas Sarwani adds, “On receipt of these farmans, the Afghans of Roh came like locusts to join the service of Sultan Bahlul.”

        This may be an exaggeration. However, the incursion of a large number of Afghans not only enabled Bahlul to defeat the Sharqis, but it also changed the complexion of Muslim society in India, making the Afghans a significant element in both south and north India.

        The most important Lodi sultan was Sikandar Lodi (1489-1517). A contemporary of Mahmud Begarha of Gujarat and Rana Sanga of Mewar, Sikandar Lodi geared the kingdom of Delhi for the coming struggle with these states. He tried to subdue the Afghan sardars, who had a strong sense of tribal independence and were not accustomed to look upon the sultan as more than a first among equals. Sikandar made the nobles stand before him to impress them with his superior status. When a royal order was sent, all the nobles had to come out of the town to receive it with due honour. All those who held jagirs had to submit accounts regularly. Drastic punishments were given to those who embezzled money or were corrupt. Sikandar Lodi had limited success in his efforts to control the nobles. Upon his death, Bahlul Lodi had divided the kingdom among his sons and relations. Though Sikandar had been able to undo this after a hard struggle, the idea of a partition of the empire among the sons of the ruler persisted among the Afghans.

        Sikandar Lodi was able to establish efficient administration in his kingdom. He laid great emphasis on justice, and all the highways of the empire were made safe from robbers and bandits. The prices of all essential commodities were remarkably cheap. The sultan took a keen interest in agriculture, abolished the octroi duty on grains, and established a new measurement of the yard, called the gaz-i-Sikandari, which continued to prevail until the Mughal times. The rent rolls prepared during his reign formed the basis for the rent rolls of Sher Shah later on.

        Sikandar Lodi is regarded as an orthodox, even a bigoted king. He sternly forbade Muslims from following practices that were against the Sharia (Islamic law), such as women visiting the graves of saints or processions being taken out in memory of saints. He re-imposed the jizya on Hindus and executed a Brahman for holding that the Hindu and Muslim scriptures were equally sacred. He also demolished a few well-known Hindu temples during his campaigns, such as the temple at Nagarkot.

        Sikandar Lodi gave magnificent grants to scholars, philosophers, and men of letters, so that cultured people from all climes and countries, including Arabia and Iran, flocked to his court. Due to the sultan’s efforts, many Sanskrit works were translated into Persian. He was also interested in music and had a number of rare Sanskrit works on music translated into Persian. During his reign, many Hindus learned Persian and were recruited to various administrative posts.

        Thus, the process of cultural rapprochement between Hindus and Muslims continued during his reign. Sikandar Lodi also extended his dominions by conquering Dholpur and Gwalior. During these operations, Sikandar Lodi, after careful survey and deliberation, selected the site for the city of Agra (1506). The town was meant to command the area of eastern Rajasthan and the route to Malwa and Gujarat. Over time, Agra became a large town and the second capital of the Lodis.

        The growing interest of Sikandar in eastern Rajasthan and Malwa was shown by his taking the Khan of Nagaur under his protection and by trying to make Ranthambhor transfer its allegiance from Malwa to Delhi. His successor, Ibrahim Lodi, even led a campaign against Mewar, which was repulsed. The growing power of the Rana in Malwa, and the extension of his power towards Agra and Bayana, presaged a conflict between Mewar and the Lodis. It is difficult to say what the outcome of this conflict would have been if Babur had not intervened.

        Kashmir
            An account of north India in the fifteenth century would be incomplete without mentioning the Kingdom of Kashmir. The beautiful valley of Kashmir was for long a forbidden land for all outsiders. According to al-Biruni, entry into Kashmir was not allowed even to Hindus who were not known personally to the nobles there. During this period, Kashmir was known as a centre of Saivism. However, the situation changed with the end of Hindu rule around the middle of the fourteenth century. The devastating attack on Kashmir in 1320 by the Mongol leader, Dalucha, was a prelude to this. It is said that Dalucha ordered a wholesale massacre of men, while women and children were enslaved and sold to the merchants of Central Asia. The towns and villages were ravaged, plundered, and set on fire. The hapless Kashmir government could offer no opposition to these doings, thereby losing all public sympathy and support.

            One hundred years after the Mongol invasion, Zainul Abidin, considered the greatest of the Muslim monarchs of Kashmir, ascended the throne. Kashmir society had profoundly changed during this period. There had been continuous incursions of Muslim saints and refugees from Central Asia into Kashmir, with the Baramulla route providing easy access. Another development was the rise of a series of remarkable Sufi saints called Rishis, who combined some features of Hinduism and Islam. Partly through the preaching of the saints and partly by force, the lower-class population had been converted to Islam. To complete the process, a vehement persecution of the Brahmins began in the reign of Sikandar Shah (1389-1413). The sultan ordered that all Brahmins and learned Hindus should either convert to Islam or leave the valley. Their temples were to be destroyed, and the idols of gold and silver were to be melted down for currency. It is said that these orders were issued at the behest of the king’s minister, Suha Bhatt, who had converted to Islam and was bent on harassing his former co-religionists.

            This situation changed with the accession of Zainul Abidin (1420-1470), who had all these orders cancelled. He reconciled and brought back to Kashmir all the non-Muslims who had fled. Those who wanted to revert to Hinduism, or had pretended to be Muslims to save their lives, were given freedom to do as they pleased. He even restored the libraries and land grants that the Hindus had enjoyed. The temples were also restored. More than one hundred years later, Abul Fazl noted that Kashmir had one hundred and fifty majestic temples. It is likely that most of these had been restored under Zainul Abidin.

            Zainul Abidin continued the policy of broad toleration in other areas as well. He abolished the siyah (tax on non-Muslims) and cow slaughter, and to respect the wishes of the Hindus, withdrew the ban on sati. Hindus occupied many high offices in his government. Thus, Suha Bhatt was appointed minister of justice and court physician. His first two queens were Hindus, being the daughters of the Raja of Jammu. They were the mothers of all his four sons. He married a third wife after the death of his first two wives.

            The Sultan was himself a learned man and composed poetry. He was well-versed in Persian, Kashmiri, Sanskrit, and Tibetan languages. He gave patronage to Sanskrit and Persian scholars and, at his instance, many Sanskrit works, such as the Mahabharata and Kalhana’s history of Kashmir, Rajatarangini, were translated into Persian and brought up to date. He was fond of music, and upon hearing of this, the Raja of Gwalior sent him two rare Sanskrit works on music.

            The Sultan also looked after the economic development of Kashmir. He sent two persons to Samarkand to learn the arts of papermaking and bookbinding. He fostered many crafts in Kashmir, such as stone-cutting and polishing, bottle-making, gold-beating, etc. He also encouraged the art of shawl-making, for which Kashmir is so famous. Musket-making and the art of manufacturing fireworks also developed in Kashmir. The Sultan advanced agriculture by constructing large numbers of dams, canals, and bridges. He was an enthusiastic builder, and his greatest engineering achievement was Zaina Lanka—the artificial island in the Wular Lake on which he built his palace and a mosque.

            Zainul Abidin is still called Bud Shah (the Great Sultan) by the Kashmiris. Though not a great warrior, he defeated the Mongol invasion of Ladakh, conquered the Baltistan area (called Tibbat-i-buzarg), and kept control over Jammu, Rajouri, etc. He thus unified the Kashmiri kingdom.

            The fame of Zainul Abidin spread far and wide. He was in touch with the leading rulers of other parts of India, as well as the leading rulers of Asia.

            A quick survey of developments during the fifteenth century shows that the regional balance of power could neither give peace nor stability. The regional kingdoms, however, had many cultural contributions to their credit. In these kingdoms, local styles of architecture were developed, often incorporating local traditions. Local languages were also patronized. While some rulers carried out large-scale destruction of temples and tried to present themselves as orthodox Muslim kings, overall, in these kingdoms, the forces of mutual accommodation and cultural integration remained active, with some rulers anticipating Akbar in a number of fields.


- Cultural Development in India (1300–1500)
    The establishment of the Delhi Sultanate at the beginning of the thirteenth century marks a new phase in the cultural development of the country. The Turkish invaders who came to India were by no means rude barbarians. Coming to West Asia during the ninth and tenth centuries from their Central Asian homelands, they had accepted Islam, just as the earlier invaders from Central Asia had accepted Buddhism and Hinduism. They had also assimilated rapidly into the culture of the area. The Arabo-Persian culture, which had embraced the Islamic lands from Morocco and Spain to Iran and its adjacent regions, was at its height at the time. The people of the region had made many important contributions in the fields of science, navigation, literature, and more. When the Turks came to India, they not only had a well-defined faith in Islam to which they were deeply attached, but they also had definite ideas about government, art, architecture, and other areas. The interaction of the Turks with the Indians, who held strong religious beliefs and had well-developed ideas of art, architecture, and literature, resulted in the long run in the development of a new, enriched culture. But the process was a long one, characterized by destruction followed or accompanied by periods of construction. Mutual misunderstanding and strife are always present when two sides hold strongly-held views. More significant, however, were the efforts at mutual understanding, which ultimately led to a process of assimilation in many fields, such as art and architecture, music, literature, and even customs, ceremonies, rituals, religious beliefs, science, and technology. However, the elements of confrontation and conflict remained strongly entrenched in both communities. The process of assimilation and convergence, therefore, had many ups and downs and varied from region to region, field to field, and period to period.

    ARCHITECTURE
        One of the first requirements of the new rulers was to have houses to live in and places of worship. They initially converted some temples and other existing buildings into mosques, while destroying many others and using their material for constructing mosques. Examples of this are the Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque near the Qutub Minar in Delhi and the building at Ajmer called Arhai Din ka Jhonpra. The former had been a temple, and the latter had been a monastery. The only new construction at Delhi was a façade of three elaborately carved arches in front of the garbhagriha, which was demolished. The style of decoration used on these arches is very interesting: no human or animal figures were used, as it was considered un-Islamic to do so. Instead, they used scrolls of flowers and verses from the Quran, which were intertwined in a very artistic manner. Soon, the Turks started constructing their own buildings. For this purpose, they mostly used indigenous craftsmen, such as stone-cutters, masons, etc., who were famous for their skills. Later, some master architects came to India from West Asia. In their buildings, the Turks used the arch and the dome on a wide scale. Neither the arch nor the dome was a Turkish or Muslim invention. The Arabs borrowed them from Rome through the Byzantine Empire, developed them, and made them their own.

        The use of the arch and the dome had a number of advantages. The dome provided a pleasing skyline, and as the architects gained more experience and confidence, the dome rose higher. Many experiments were made in placing a round dome on a square building and raising the dome higher and higher. In this way, many lofty and impressive buildings were constructed. The arch and the dome dispensed with the need for a large number of pillars to support the roof and enabled the construction of large halls with a clear view. Such places of assembly were useful in mosques as well as in palaces. However, the arch and the dome required strong cement; otherwise, the stones could not be held in place. The Turks used fine quality light mortar in their buildings. Thus, new architectural forms and superior mortar became widespread in northern India with the arrival of the Turks.

        The arch and the dome were known to the Indians earlier, but they were not used on a large scale. Moreover, the correct scientific method of constructing the arch was rarely employed. The architectural device generally used by the Indians consisted of placing one stone over another, narrowing the gap until it could be covered by a coping stone or by placing a beam over a slab of stones. The Turkish rulers used both the dome and arch method as well as the slab and beam method in their buildings.

        In the sphere of decoration, the Turks eschewed the representation of human and animal figures in their buildings. Instead, they used geometrical and floral designs, combining them with panels of inscriptions containing verses from the Quran. Thus, the Arabic script itself became a work of art. The combination of these decorative devices was called arabesque. They also freely borrowed Hindu motifs such as the bell motif, the leaf motif, swastika, lotus, etc. Thus, like the Indians, the Turks were intensely fond of decoration. The skill of Indian stone-cutters was fully utilized for this purpose. The walls of the small tomb of Iltutmish (near the Qutub Minar, Delhi) were so intricately carved that hardly a square inch is left vacant. The Turks also added color to their buildings by using red sandstone. Yellow sandstone or marble was used in these buildings for decoration and to highlight the color of red sandstone.

        The most magnificent building constructed by the Turks in the thirteenth century was the Qutub Minar. This tapering tower, originally 71.4 meters high, was begun by Aibak and completed by Iltutmish. It is incorrect to think that it was dedicated to the Sufi saint Qutb-ud-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki, the venerated saint of Delhi, since it was not called the Qutub Minar at the time but the Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque. Although traditions of building towers are found both in India and West Asia, the Qutub Minar is unique in many ways. It derives its effect mainly from the skillful manner in which the balconies have been projected yet linked with the main tower, the use of red and white sandstone, and marble in panels and in the top stages, and the ribbed effect.

        The Khalji period saw significant building activity. Alauddin built his capital at Siri, a few kilometers away from the site around the Qutub. Unfortunately, hardly anything of this city survives now. Alauddin planned a tower twice the height of the Qutub but did not live to complete it. However, he added an entrance door to the Qutub. This door, known as the Alai Darwaza, has arches of very pleasing proportions. It also contains a dome, which, for the first time, was built on correct scientific lines. Thus, the art of building the arch and the dome on scientific lines had been mastered by Indian craftsmen by this time.

        There was great building activity during the Tughlaq period, which marked both the climax of the Delhi Sultanate and the beginning of its decline. Ghiyasuddin and Muhammad Tughlaq built the huge palace-fortress complex called Tughlaqabad. By blocking the passage of the Yamuna, a huge artificial lake was created around it. The tomb of Ghiyasuddin marks a new trend in architecture. To achieve a good skyline, the building was placed upon a high platform. Its beauty was heightened by a marble dome.

        A striking feature of Tughlaq architecture was the sloping walls. This is called ‘batter,’ and it gives the effect of strength and solidity to the building. However, we do not find any ‘batter’ in the buildings of Firuz Tughlaq. A second feature of Tughlaq architecture was the deliberate attempt to combine the principles of the arch and the lintel and beam in their buildings. This is clearly visible in the buildings of Firuz Tughlaq. The Hauz Khas was a pleasure resort and had a huge lake around it. It also had a madrasa. The same features are found in some buildings of Firuz Shah’s new fort, now called the Kotla. The Tughlaqs generally did not use the costly red sandstone in their buildings but the cheaper and more easily available grey stone. Since it was not easy to carve this type of stone, the Tughlaq buildings have minimal decoration. However, the decorative device found in all the buildings of Firuz is the lotus.

        Many grand mosques were also built during this period. It is not possible to describe all of them here. What is worth noting is that, by this time, an independent style of architecture had emerged in India, combining many of the new devices brought by the Turks with the indigenous forms. The Lodis developed this tradition further. Both the arch and the lintel and beam were used in their buildings. Balconies, kiosks, and eaves of the Rajasthani-Gujarati style were also used. Another device used by the Lodis was placing their buildings, especially tombs, on a high platform, thus giving the building a sense of size as well as a better skyline. Some of the tombs were placed in the midst of gardens. The Lodi Garden in Delhi is a fine example of this. Some of the tombs were octagonal in shape. Many of these features were later adopted by the Mughals, culminating in the Taj Mahal built by Shah Jahan.

        By the time of the breakup of the Delhi Sultanate, individual styles of architecture had also developed in the various kingdoms in different parts of India. Many of these, again, were powerfully influenced by local architectural traditions. As we have seen, this happened in Bengal, Gujarat, Malwa, the Deccan, etc. During the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, the architectural style evolved in Delhi under the Tughlaqs, which was carried forward and modified in the various regional kingdoms. Thus, there was an outburst of building activity, marked by the growth of many styles of architecture in different parts of the country.

    Religious Traditions and Beliefs
        Islam was not a stranger in India when the Turks established their empire in northern India. Islam had been established in Sindh since the eighth century and in Punjab from the tenth century. Arab travelers had settled in Kerala between the eighth and tenth centuries. During this period, Arab travelers and Sufi saints traveled to different parts of India. Al-Biruni’s book Kitab-ul-Hind and other writings had familiarized the learned sections in West Asia with Hindu ideas and beliefs. As noted earlier, Buddhist texts, Indian fables, and books on astronomy and medicine had been translated into Arabic. Visits by Indian yogis to the region were not uncommon. The influence of Buddhism and Vedantic ideas on Islamic thinking has been a subject of considerable debate among scholars. Remnants of Buddhist monasteries, stupas, and images of the Buddha found in Afghanistan and parts of Central Asia, particularly along old trade routes, show the extent of Buddhist influence in these areas at one time. While it is difficult to determine the precise extent of the influence of Indian philosophical ideas, it is hardly disputable that both Greek and Indian ideas, in varying proportions, made a definite contribution to the development of Islamic philosophy in its formative phase. These ideas provided the background to the rise of the Sufi movement, which, after its establishment in India in the twelfth century, influenced both Muslims and Hindus, providing a common platform for the two. However, scholars believe that while various rituals and practices, including yogic practices, were freely drawn from Hinduism by the early Sufis and assimilated into their system, their basic ideological structure remained Islamic.

    The Sufi Movement
        The tenth century is significant in Islamic history for a variety of reasons: it marks the rise of the Turks on the ruins of the Abbasid Caliphate, as well as important changes in the realm of ideas and beliefs. In the realm of ideas, it marks the end of the domination of the Mutazila or rationalist philosophy, and the rise of orthodox schools based on the Quran and Hadith (traditions of the Prophet and his companions) and of the Sufi mystic orders. The ‘rationalists’ had been accused of spreading skepticism and atheism. In particular, it was argued that their philosophy of monism, which held that God and the created world were fundamentally one, was heretical on the grounds that it abolished the difference between the creator and the created.

        The works of the ‘traditionalists’ crystallized into four schools of Islamic law. Of these, the Hanafi school, which was the most liberal, was adopted by the eastern Turks who later came to India.

        Mystics, known as Sufis, had risen in Islam at a very early stage. Most of them were persons of deep devotion who were disgusted by the vulgar display of wealth and degeneration of morals following the establishment of the Islamic empire. Hence, these saints wanted nothing to do with the state—a tradition which continued later on. Some of the early Sufis, such as the woman mystic Rabia (d. eighth century) and Mansur bin Hallaj (d. tenth century), placed great emphasis on love as the bond between God and the individual soul. However, their pantheistic approach led them into conflict with the orthodox elements, who had Mansur executed for heresy. Despite this setback, mystic ideas continued to spread among the Muslim masses.

        Al-Ghazzali (d. 1112), who is venerated both by the orthodox elements and the Sufis, tried to reconcile mysticism with Islamic orthodoxy. He succeeded to a large extent. He further discredited the ‘rationalist’ philosophy by arguing that positive knowledge of God and His qualities cannot be gained by reason but only by revelation. Thus, the revealed book, the Quran, was vital for a mystic.

        Around this time, the Sufis were organized into 12 orders or silsilahs. A silsilah was generally led by a prominent mystic who lived in a khangah or hospice with his disciples. The link between the teacher or pir and his disciples or murid was a vital part of the Sufi system. Every pir nominated a successor or walt to carry on his work.

        The monastic organization of the Sufis, and some of their practices such as penance, fasting, and holding the breath, are sometimes traced to Buddhist and Hindu yogic influences. Buddhism was widely prevalent in Central Asia before the advent of Islam, and the legend of the Buddha as a saintly man had passed into Islamic legend. Yogis continued to visit West Asia even after the advent of Islam, and the yogic book Amrit-kund had been translated into Persian from Sanskrit. Thus, Hindu and Buddhist practices and rituals seem to have been absorbed and assimilated by the Sufis even before they came to India. Whether Buddhist philosophical ideas and Vedantic ideas had, in a significant manner, influenced Sufism is a matter of controversy. The origin of ideas is difficult to trace. The Sufi saints and many modern thinkers trace Sufi ideas to the Quran. What is important to note here is that, irrespective of origin, there were many similarities in the ideas of the Sufis and Hindu yogis and mystics regarding the nature of God, His relationship with the soul, and the material world. This provided a basis for mutual toleration and understanding. The humane spirit of Sufism is well expressed by Sanai, a leading Persian poet of the time:

        “Faith and infidelity, both are galloping on the way towards Him;
        And are exclaiming (together): He is one and none share His kingdom.”

        The Sufi orders are broadly divided into two: Ba-shura, which followed Islamic law (shara), and de-shara, which were not bound by it. Both types of orders prevailed in India, with the latter being followed more by wandering saints. Although these saints did not establish an order, some became figures of popular veneration, often for both Muslims and Hindus alike.

    The Chishti and Suharwardi Silsilahs
        Of the ba-shura movements, only two acquired significant influence and following in northern India during the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries. These were the Chishti and Suharwardi silsilahs. The Chishti order was established in India by Khwaja Muinuddin Chishti, who came to India around 1192, shortly after the defeat and death of Prithvi Raj Chauhan. After staying for some time in Lahore and Delhi, he finally settled in Ajmer, which was an important political center and already had a sizable Muslim population. No authentic record of his activities is available; he did not write any books, but his fame rose along with that of his successors. Among the disciples of Shaikh Muinuddin (d. 1235) were Bakhtiyar Kaki and his disciple Farid-ud-Din Ganj-i-Shakar. Farid-ud-Din confined his activities to Hansi and Ajodhan (in modern Haryana and the Punjab, respectively). He was deeply respected in Delhi, so much so that streams of people would throng around him whenever he visited Delhi. His outlook was so broad and humane that some of his verses were later found quoted in the Adi-Granth of the Sikhs.

        The most famous of the Chishti saints, however, were Nizamuddin Auliya and Nasiruddin Chiragh-i-Delhi. These early Sufis mingled freely with people of the lower classes, including Hindus.
