        The nobility, along with the landed gentry, the zamindars, formed what may be called the ruling class in medieval India. Socially and economically, the Mughal nobility formed a privileged class. Theoretically, the doors of the Mughal nobility were open to everyone. In practice, persons belonging to aristocratic families, whether they were Indians or foreigners, had a decided advantage.

        To begin with, the bulk of the Mughal nobles were drawn from the homeland of the Mughals—Turan—and from its neighboring areas, such as Tajikistan, Khurasan, and Iran. Although Babur was a Turk, the Mughal rulers never followed a narrow racist policy. Babur tried to win the leading Afghan nobles to his side, but they proved to be restless and untrustworthy and soon defected. The tussle between the Mughals and the Afghans continued in Bihar and Bengal even under Akbar. However, from the time of Jahangir, more Afghans began to be recruited into the nobility. Indian Muslims, known as Shaikhzadas or Hindustanis, were also given service.

        From the time of Akbar, Hindus also began to be inducted into the nobility on a regular basis. The largest section among them consisted of the Rajputs. Initially, among the Rajputs, the Kachhwahas predominated. According to modern calculations, the proportion of Hindus in the nobility under Akbar in 1594 was about 16 percent. However, these figures do not provide an adequate idea of the position and influence of Hindus. Both Raja Man Singh and Raja Birbal were personal friends and close companions of Akbar, while in revenue administration, Raja Todar Mal held a place of great influence and honor. The Rajputs who were recruited to the nobility were either hereditary rajas or belonged to aristocratic families related to or allied with the raja. Thus, their incorporation into the nobility strengthened its aristocratic character.

        Despite this, the nobility did provide an avenue of promotion and distinction to individuals from the lower sections of society. Many Kayasthas and Khatris were employed at various levels in the central and provincial governments, and a few of them were elevated to the position of nobles. Some individuals of humble origins even attained the status of mansabdars.

        The nobility attained considerable stability under Jahangir and Shah Jahan. Both these monarchs paid careful attention to the organization of the nobility through the mansabdari system. Rules and regulations were established for orderly promotions, discipline, and the recruitment of competent people into the imperial service.

        The Mughal nobles, as mentioned earlier, received salaries that were extremely high by any standards. This, along with the liberal policy of the Mughal emperors in matters of faith and the stable political conditions in India, attracted many talented individuals from foreign lands to the Mughal court. As a result, there was a reverse brain drain. Due to the influx of Iranis, Turanis, and others in search of service at the Mughal court, a French traveler, Bernier, remarked that the Mughal nobility consisted of “foreigners who enticed each other to the court.” However, modern research has shown this statement to be inaccurate. While talented individuals continued to migrate to India and rise to prominence in Mughal service, they all settled in India permanently. Thus, just like in earlier periods, India continued to provide a home for many people from outside. These immigrants quickly assimilated into Indian society and culture while retaining some of their distinctive traits. This contributed to the richness and diversity of Indian culture.

        Under Jahangir and Shah Jahan, the majority of the nobles were already those born in India. Simultaneously, the proportion of Afghans, Indian Muslims (Hindustanis), and Hindus in the nobility continued to rise. A new section among the Hindus that entered the nobility during this period were the Marathas. Jahangir was the first monarch to recognize the importance of the Marathas in the Deccan and sought to win them over to his side. This policy was continued by Shah Jahan. Among the Maratha sardars who served Shah Jahan was Shahji, the father of Shivaji, although he later defected. Aurangzeb also recruited many Marathas and Deccani Muslims into his service.

        We shall discuss Mughal relations with the Marathas in a later section. However, it is worth noting that while Hindus formed roughly 24 percent of the nobility under Shah Jahan, their proportion rose to about 33 percent during the second half of Aurangzeb’s reign, with their total number increasing four and a half times. Among the Hindu nobles, the Marathas made up more than half during Aurangzeb’s reign.

        Although the Mughal nobles received extremely high salaries, their expenses were also substantial. Each noble maintained a large retinue of servants and attendants, as well as a sizable stable of horses, elephants, and other forms of transport. Many of them maintained large harems, which was considered normal for a man of status in those times. The nobles imitated the Mughal emperors in leading a highly ostentatious lifestyle. They lived in magnificent houses with gardens containing fruit trees and running water. They wore the finest clothes and spent lavishly on their meals. According to one account, 40 dishes were prepared for each of Akbar’s meals. A significant amount was spent on fruits, with the choicest varieties being imported from Samarqand and Bokhara.

        Ice, which was considered a luxury, was used year-round by the privileged classes. Jewelry and ornaments, worn by both men and women, were another major expenditure. Jahangir introduced the fashion of men wearing expensive earrings after piercing their ears. To some extent, jewelry also served as a financial reserve for emergencies. Another major expense was the mandatory presents to the emperor, which had to be given twice a year. However, the value of these gifts was fixed according to the individual’s status, and in return, nobles received gifts from the emperor.

        Although extravagant spending rather than hoarding was the dominant characteristic of the ruling class, only a few nobles managed to remain debt-free and leave large inheritances for their children. Despite this, the nobility contributed significantly to the economy, both directly and indirectly. Many nobles purchased land or received land grants from the emperor, settling in these areas and developing orchards or building covered markets (mandis) that generated rental and sales income. They also lent money on interest to traders or engaged in trade, often in the name of merchants or in partnership with them. In a remarkable passage, Abul Fazl advised nobles to “invest a little in commercial speculation and engage in remunerative undertakings.” Although Islamic law condemned usury, Abul Fazl encouraged nobles to invest money on interest, reflecting contemporary values.

        It is difficult to determine the precise share of the nobility in the commercial enterprises of the period. At times, some nobles, including royal princes, misused their positions to monopolize the sale and purchase of certain goods or to coerce artisans and traders into selling their goods and services at lower prices. However, such instances were not widespread enough to significantly impact trade, commerce, or artistic production.

        An English factor at Surat remarked in 1614 that “large and small are merchants.” Even members of the royal family, including princes, princesses, and royal ladies, participated in trade by freighting goods on foreign ships or even owning ships themselves. Mir Jumla, a prominent nobleman during Aurangzeb’s reign, owned a fleet of ships that carried out extensive trade with Persia, Arabia, and Southeast Asia. The lure of commercial profits reached such an extent that even Aurangzeb’s chief qazi had significant commercial interests, which he attempted to conceal from the emperor. Thus, the Mughal nobility had a number of unusual features.

        Though often divided along ethnic lines, the Mughal nobility formed a composite ruling class representing different regions and religions. It also sought to promote a shared culture by extending patronage to painters, musicians, poets—both Persian and Hindawi—and scholars. Though essentially feudal in character, as land was its primary source of income, it had also developed many characteristics of a bureaucracy. Additionally, it was becoming increasingly commerce- and money-minded.

        Thus, the Mughal state and ruling class did not act as a barrier to India’s economic development. Whether this development could, by itself, have led India toward a capitalist path is debatable, but we need not enter into that discussion here. Our main concern is to examine whether the economy continued to grow during this period and, if so, the direction of its development.

        The rapid expansion of the nobility during the seventeenth century, tensions between different groups, individuals, and factions, and a crisis in the functioning of the sagirdari system had an adverse effect on discipline and the effective operation of the nobility under Aurangzeb and his successors. Some of these aspects are examined in greater detail in a subsequent chapter.

    Zamindars and the Rural Gentry
        From the writings of Abul Fazl and other contemporary authors, it is clear that personal ownership of land had existed in India for a long time. The right of ownership in land primarily depended on heredity, but new rights of ownership were constantly being created. The tradition was that anyone who first brought land under cultivation was considered its owner. There was plenty of cultivable wasteland (banjar) available in medieval times. It was not difficult for an enterprising group of people to settle a new village or bring wastelands belonging to a village under cultivation and become the owners of these lands. In addition to owning the lands they cultivated, a significant section of the zamindars had the hereditary right to collect land revenue from a number of villages. This was called their taluqa or zamindari. For collecting the land revenue, the zamindars received a share, which could go up to 25% in some areas. However, the zamindar was not the owner of all the land in his zamindari. The peasants who actually cultivated the land could not be dispossessed as long as they paid the land revenue. Thus, both the zamindars and the peasants had their own hereditary rights in land.

        Above the zamindars were the rajas, who dominated larger or smaller territories and enjoyed varying degrees of internal autonomy. These rajas were also referred to as zamindars by Persian writers to emphasize their subordinate status, but their position was superior to that of the ordinary zamindars who collected land revenue. Thus, medieval society, including rural society, was highly segmented and hierarchical.

        The zamindars, rajas, and chiefs had their own armed forces and generally lived in forts or garhis, which served as both places of refuge and status symbols. The combined forces of these groups, collectively called zamindars in medieval sources, were significant. According to the Ain-i-Akbari, during Akbar’s reign, they possessed 3,84,558 cavalry, 42,77,057 foot soldiers, 1,863 elephants, and 4,260 cannons. However, since the zamindars were dispersed, they could never field such large forces at any one time or in a single location.

        The zamindars generally had close connections with the peasants in their zamindaris, often based on caste, clan, or tribal ties. They also had considerable local knowledge about land productivity. The zamindars formed a powerful and numerous class, found throughout the country under different names such as deshmukh, patil, nayak, etc. Thus, it was not easy for any central authority to ignore or alienate them.

        It is difficult to determine the exact living standards of the zamindars. Compared to the nobles, their income was limited; the smaller zamindars may have lived much like the peasants. However, the larger zamindars likely had living standards similar to those of petty rajas or nobles. Most of the zamindars lived in the countryside, forming a loosely organized, dispersed rural gentry.

        It would be incorrect to view the zamindars merely as those who fought for control over land and exploited cultivators. Many zamindars had close caste and kinship ties with the landowning cultivating castes in their zamindaris. They not only set social standards but also provided capital and organization for establishing new villages and expanding or improving cultivation.

    The Middle Strata
        There has been much discussion on whether medieval India had a middle class. The French traveler Bernier claimed that India had no “middle state”—a person was either extremely rich or lived in misery. However, this statement is not entirely accurate. If the term middle class refers to traders and shopkeepers, then India had a large class of wealthy traders and merchants, some of whom were among the richest in the world at that time. These merchants had traditional rights, including protection of life and property. However, unlike their European counterparts, they did not have the right to administer towns. In Europe, merchants acquired such rights under special circumstances, but these were often curtailed whenever strong territorial states emerged, as in France and Britain.

        If middle state refers to a section of society whose standard of living was between the rich and the poor, then such groups were indeed present in Mughal India. These included small mansabdars, petty shopkeepers, and a small but significant class of master craftsmen. It also comprised professionals such as hakims (physicians), leading musicians and artists, historians, scholars, qazis (judges), theologians, and a large number of petty officials or clerks who managed the vast Mughal administrative machinery. While petty officials were generally paid in cash and supplemented their income through corruption, many others, especially scholars and religious figures, were granted small landholdings for sustenance. These grants were known as madad-i-maash in Mughal terminology and sasan in Rajasthan. Such grants were typically renewed by each ruler but often became hereditary in practice. These individuals often formed part of the rural gentry, serving as a link between villages and towns. Writers, historians, and theologians frequently came from this class. However, the middle strata did not constitute a single class; their interests varied significantly. They were also drawn from various religious and caste groups, further emphasizing the diversity of medieval Indian society.

    ORGANIZATION OF TRADE AND COMMERCE
        The Indian trading classes were large in numbers, spread out all over the country, well organized, and highly professional. Some specialized in long-distance, inter-regional trade, and some in local, retail trade. The former were called seth, bora, or modi, while the latter were called deoparis or banik. In addition to retailing goods, the baniks had their own agents in the villages and townships, with whose help they purchased foodgrains and cash crops. There was a special class of traders, the banjaras, who specialized in carrying bulk goods. The banjaras used to move over long distances, sometimes with thousands of oxen carrying foodgrains, pulses, ghee, salt, etc. The more expensive goods, such as textiles, silks, etc., were laden on camels and mules or in carts. But it was cheaper to move bulk goods through the rivers on boats. Boat traffic on waterways and coastal trade along the seashore were more highly developed than now. The trade in foodstuffs and a wide range of textile products were the most important components of inter-regional trade during the period. Bengal exported sugar and rice as well as delicate muslin and silk. The coast of Coromandel had become a center for textile production and had a brisk trade with Gujarat, both along the coast and across the Deccan. Gujarat was the entry point of foreign goods. It exported fine textiles and silks (patola) to North India, with Burhanpur and Agra as the two nodal points of trade. It received foodgrains and silk from Bengal and also imported pepper from Malabar. North India imported luxury items and also exported indigo and foodgrains. Lahore was another center of handicraft production. It was also the distribution center for the luxury products of Kashmir—shawls, carpets, etc. The products of Punjab and Sindh moved down the river Indus. It had close trade links with Kabul and Qandahar on the one hand and with Delhi and Agra on the other.

        It will thus be seen that India’s inter-regional trade was not in luxuries alone. The movement of these goods was made possible by complex networks linking wholesalers with merchants down to the regional and local levels through agents (gumashtas) and commission agents (dalals). The Dutch and English traders who came to Gujarat during the seventeenth century found the Indian traders to be active and alert. There was keen competition for inside information, and whenever there was demand for goods in one part of the country, it was rapidly met.

        Movement of goods was also facilitated by the growth of a financial system that permitted easy transmission of money from one part of the country to another. This was done through the use of hundis. The hundi was a letter of credit payable after a period of time at a discount. The hundis often included insurance, which was charged at different rates based on the value of the goods, destination, means of transport (land, river, or sea), etc. The sarrafs (shroffs), who specialized in changing money, also dealt with hundis. In the process, they acted as private banks: they kept money in deposit from the nobles and lent it. By means of hundis, they created credit, which supplemented the money in circulation, since the merchant could cash his hundi after he had sold his goods at the point of his destination. Thus, movement of money, which was always a risky enterprise, could be reduced, especially when rich traders such as Virji Vohra set up agency houses in different parts of India, and also in West Asia and Southeast Asia.

        The trading community in India did not belong to one caste or religion. The Gujarati merchants included Hindus, Jains, and Muslims, who were mostly Bohras. In Rajasthan, Oswals, Maheshwaris, and Agarwals began to be called Marwaris. Overland trade to Central Asia was in the hands of Multanis, Afghans, and Khatris. The Marwaris spread out to Maharashtra and Bengal during the eighteenth century. The Chettis on the Coromandel coast and the Muslim merchants of Malabar, both Indian and Arab, formed the most important trading communities of South India.

        The trading community in India, especially in the port towns, included some of the richest merchants, comparable in wealth and power to the merchant princes of Europe. Thus, Virji Vohra dominated the Surat trade for several decades. He owned a large fleet of ships and was reputed to be among the wealthiest men of his time. Abdul Ghafur Bohra left 55 lakh rupees in cash and goods and a fleet of 17 sea-going ships at the time of his death in 1718. Similarly, Malay Chetti of the Coromandel coast, Kashi Viranna, and Sunca Rama Chetti were reputed to be extremely wealthy and had extensive commercial dealings in India and abroad. There were many wealthy merchants at Agra, Delhi, Balasore (Orissa), and Bengal as well. Some of these merchants, especially those living in the coastal towns, lived ostentatiously and imitated the manners of the nobles.

        European travelers mention the commodious and well-built houses in which the wealthy merchants of Agra and Delhi lived. But the ordinary merchants lived in houses above their shops. The French traveler, Bernier, says that merchants tried to look poor because they feared being treated like "filled sponges," i.e., squeezed of their wealth. This does not appear to be fully correct. Emperors from the time of Sher Shah passed many laws for protecting the property of merchants. The laws of Sher Shah are well known. Jahangir’s ordinances included a provision that if anyone, whether unbeliever or Muslim, should die, his property and effects should be left for his heirs, and no one should interfere with them. If he had no heirs, inspectors and separate guardians would be appointed to guard the property so that its value might be expended in lawful expenditures, such as building mosques and sarais, repairing broken bridges, and digging tanks and wells. However, local officials could always abuse their power to harass traders. Despite some harassment, the property of merchants was generally safe. Means of transport were cheap and adequate for their needs. Despite complaints by some European travelers, safety on the roads was satisfactory and could be covered by insurance. The means of travel, with sarais at distances of 5 kos on principal highways, was as good as in Europe at the time. Nevertheless, trade and traders continued to have a low social status. The influence of merchants on political processes is a matter of controversy. Merchants in India were not without influence in political quarters where their interests were concerned. Thus, each community of merchants had its leader or nagarseth, who could intercede with local officials on their behalf. We do have instances of strikes (hartal) by merchants in Ahmedabad and elsewhere to press their points of view. We have also noted the involvement of members of the Mughal royal family and prominent nobles, such as Mir Jumla, in trade. Thus, the Mughal ruling class was not unconcerned with business and the protection of commercial interests, though it was not as actively involved in pushing its business interests as some European states such as Britain, France, and Holland were.

        Trade and commerce expanded in India during the seventeenth century due to several factors. An important factor was the political integration of the country under Mughal rule and the establishment of conditions of law and order over extensive areas. The Mughals paid attention to roads and sarais. Taxes were levied on goods at the point of their entry into the empire. Road cesses or rahdari was declared illegal, though some local rajas continued to collect it. The Mughals minted silver rupees of high purity from mints scattered all over the empire. Any trader could carry silver to the royal mint and have coins in exchange on payment of a batta (discount). The Mughal rupee became a standard coin in India and abroad, thus helping India’s trade.

        Some of the Mughal policies also helped in the commercialization of the economy, or the growth of a money economy. Salaries for the standing army as well as many of the administrative personnel (but not the nobles) were paid in cash. Under the zabti system, land revenue was assessed and required to be paid in cash. Even when the peasant was given the option of choosing other methods of assessment, such as crop-sharing, the state’s share was generally sold in the villages with the help of grain dealers. It has been estimated that about 20 percent of the rural produce was marketed, which was a high proportion. The growth of rural grain markets led to the rise of small townships or qasbas. The demand for all types of luxury goods by the nobles led to the expansion of handicraft production and the growth of towns.

        Already during the sixteenth century, a number of major towns had developed in the country. According to Ralph Fitch, Agra and Fatehpur Sikri were larger than London, then one of the biggest towns in Europe. Monserrate, the Jesuit priest who came to Akbar’s court, said that Lahore was second to none among the cities of Europe or Asia. A recent study shows that Agra more than doubled in size during the seventeenth century. Bernier, who wrote in the middle of the seventeenth century, stated that Delhi was not smaller than Paris and that Agra was bigger than Delhi. During this period, Ahmadnagar and Burhanpur in the west, Multan in the northwest, and Patna, Rajmahal, and Dacca in the east grew to become large towns. Ahmedabad was as large as London and its suburbs, and Patna had a population of two lakhs—a large size by the standards of those times. These towns were not only administrative centers but also developed as centers of trade and manufacture.

        The Mughal ability to collect a high share of the rural produce, which was commuted into money and concentrated in the hands of the nobility, stimulated the demand for all kinds of luxury goods, including building materials for residential houses, sarais, baolis, etc. The growth of arms manufacturing—guns of all types, cannons, armor, etc.—and shipbuilding were two primary examples of direct government intervention in these industries. Both Akbar and Aurangzeb were deeply interested in the manufacture of guns of all types, including mobile guns, and took steps to improve their production. Indian steel swords were also in demand outside India.

        In 1651, Shah Jahan initiated a program for building sea-going vessels, and four to six ships were built each year for voyages to West Asia. In the following year, six ships were put into commission. This was part of a shipbuilding program led by many wealthy merchants and nobles. As a result, Indian shipyards were soon in a position to produce ships based on European models, and freight rates to West Asia were reduced.

    Foreign Trade and the European Traders
        We have already mentioned that there were several ports and towns from which brisk trade between India and the outer world was carried on. India not only supplied foodstuffs, such as sugar and rice, to many countries of Southeast and West Asia, but Indian textiles also played a very important role in the trade of the region. As an English agent observed, “From Aden to Achin, from head to foot, everyone was clothed in Indian textiles.” This statement, although slightly exaggerated (as Egypt and Ottoman Turkey also produced cotton and exported textiles), was essentially true. This made India the virtual manufactory of the Asian world (excluding China). The only articles India needed to import were certain metals, such as tin and copper, whose production was insufficient (tin was used for making bronze), some spices for food and medicinal purposes, warhorses, and luxury items (such as ivory). The favorable balance of trade was met by the import of gold and silver.

        As a result of the expansion of India’s foreign trade, the import of silver and gold into India increased during the seventeenth century. So much so that Bernier remarked, “Gold and silver, after circulating over every part of the world, is finally buried in India, which is the sink of gold and silver.” This statement is also somewhat exaggerated because, in those days, every country tried to hold on to its gold and silver. However, India and China were more successful in doing so due to the scale of their economies and their largely self-sufficient nature.

        We have already discussed the advent of the Portuguese in India towards the end of the fifteenth century. During the seventeenth century, many other European traders, especially the Dutch, the English, and later the French, came to India for trade. This enterprise was a direct result of the growth of the European economy, which followed a rapid expansion in agriculture and manufacturing.

        The Portuguese power had begun to decline during the second half of the sixteenth century. Despite vehement Portuguese opposition, the Dutch established themselves at Masulipatam, obtaining a farman from the ruler of Golconda in 1606. They also established themselves in the Spice Islands (Java and Sumatra), and by 1610, they dominated the spice trade. The Dutch originally came to the coast for the spice trade but quickly realized that spices could be obtained most easily in exchange for Indian textiles. The cloth produced on the Coromandel Coast was the most acceptable in Southeast Asia and also the cheapest to carry. Hence, the Dutch spread south from Masulipatam to the Coromandel Coast, obtaining Pulicat from the local ruler and making it their base of operations.

        Like the Dutch, the English also came to the East for the spice trade. However, the hostility of the Dutch, who had more resources and had already established themselves in the Spice Islands, forced the English to concentrate on India. After defeating a Portuguese fleet outside Surat, they were finally able to set up a factory there in 1612. This was confirmed in 1618 by a farman from Jahangir, obtained with the help of Sir Thomas Roe. The Dutch followed, and soon they too established a factory at Surat.

        The English quickly realized the importance of Gujarat as a center for India’s export trade in textiles. They attempted to break into India’s trade with the Red Sea and the Persian Gulf ports. In 1622, with the help of the Persian forces, they captured Ormuz, the Portuguese base at the head of the Persian Gulf.

        Thus, by the first quarter of the seventeenth century, both the Dutch and the English were well established in Indian trade, and Portuguese control of the sea was broken forever. The Portuguese remained at Goa, Daman, and Diu, but their share in India’s overseas trade declined continuously.

        Recent research has shown that, despite their dominance at sea, the Europeans were never able to oust Indian traders from Asian trade. In fact, the share of European trading companies from any part of India—Gujarat, Coromandel, or Bengal—remained a fraction of India’s foreign trade. Indian traders were able to maintain their position for several reasons: when it came to the textile trade, Indian traders knew both the domestic and foreign markets better. Additionally, Indians were willing to work at lower profit margins of 10 to 15 percent, whereas the Dutch considered 40 to 50 percent as the minimum to meet their overhead costs, including the maintenance of factories and warships. The English approach must have been similar. The Dutch and the English found that they could not trade in India, or even sustain their factories, without the cooperation of the Mughal government and Indian traders. To reduce their operational costs, they started freighting Indian merchants’ goods on their ships. Indian traders had little objection to this, as it made their operations safer. At the same time, Indian shipping expanded significantly. From about 50 ships in the middle of the seventeenth century at Surat—many of them well-built—its numbers rose to at least 112 by the end of the century. This was another indicator of the growth of India’s foreign trade and domestic manufacturing.

        Apart from participating in Asian trade, the English and the Dutch searched for articles that could be exported from India to Europe. Initially, the most important trade, apart from pepper, was indigo, which was used to dye woolens. The most suitable indigo was produced at Sarkhej in Gujarat and Bayana near Agra. Soon, the English developed the export of Indian textiles, called ‘calicoes,’ to Europe. Initially, the produce of Gujarat was sufficient for this purpose. As demand grew, the English sought cloth from Agra and its surroundings. Even this was not enough, leading them to develop the Coromandel Coast as an alternative source of supply. By 1640, exports of cloth from the Coromandel equaled those from Gujarat, and by 1660, they were three times higher. Masulipatam and Fort St. George, which later became Madras, were the chief ports for this trade.

        The Dutch joined the English in this new venture, exporting both calico and indigo from the Coromandel.

        The English also explored Lahri Bandar at the mouth of the Indus River, which could draw the produce of Multan and Lahore by transporting goods down the river. However, this trade remained secondary to the Gujarat trade. More important were their efforts to develop trade in Bengal and Orissa. The activities of the Portuguese and Magh pirates in East Bengal slowed this development. However, by 1650, the English had established themselves at Hooghly and Balasore in Orissa, exporting raw silk and sugar in addition to textiles. Another major export item was saltpeter, which supplemented European sources for gunpowder and was also used as ballast for ships bound for Europe. The best quality saltpeter was found in Bihar. By the end of the century, exports from the eastern regions equaled those from the Coromandel.

        Thus, the English and Dutch companies opened new markets and export opportunities for India. Indian textiles became a rage in England by the last quarter of the seventeenth century. As an English observer wrote, “Almost everything that used to be made of wool or silk, whether related to women’s dress or household furniture, was supplied by Indian trade.” Due to agitation, in 1701, “all calicoes painted, dyed, printed, or stained” from Persia, China, or the East Indies (i.e., India) were banned. However, these and other laws imposing severe penalties had little effect. Instead of printed cloth, the export of white Indian calicoes, which had risen to 9.14 lakh pieces in 1701, jumped to 20 lakh pieces in 1719.

        Although India’s trade with Europe grew rapidly during the second half of the seventeenth and early eighteenth centuries, intra-Asian trade remained much more lucrative. It is estimated that only 14 percent of the spices produced were exported to Europe, with most being consumed in India and China. During the seventeenth century, India’s textile exports to West Asia and East Africa also grew. A new trade item, coffee, was produced in Yemen (Southern Arabia).

        Lahore and Multan were major centers for India’s overland trade. A large colony of Indian traders, amounting to 10,000, lived in different parts of Iran and spread to Bokhara, Samarkand, and even South Russia. Indian traders had colonies at Baku and Astrakhan, trading up to Moscow. There were also traders in Yarkand and Khotan (modern Xinjiang), facilitating trade from Punjab via Kashmir and Ladakh to China. However, overland trade declined with the collapse of the Safavid Empire and the disintegration of the Mughal Empire.

        The growth of India’s foreign trade, the influx of gold and silver into the country, and the linking of India more closely with the rapidly expanding European markets had a number of important consequences. While the Indian economy grew, the influx of silver and gold into the country was even faster. As a result, during the first half of the seventeenth century, prices almost doubled. The effect of this price rise on different sections of society has yet to be worked out in detail. It probably weakened the old traditional ties in the villages and made the nobility more money-minded, greedy, and demanding.

        Secondly, the European nations searched for alternatives to the export of gold and silver to India. One method was to enter the Asian trade network by trying to monopolize the spice trade and capture the Indian trade in textiles. As we have seen, they had only limited success in these fields. Hence, they tried to acquire empires in India and its neighborhood so that they could pay for the goods exported to Europe from the revenues of these territories. The Dutch were able to conquer Java and Sumatra. But the key was India. Both the English and the French competed for the conquest of India, but they could not succeed as long as India remained strong and united—first under Mughal rule and then under able provincial governors. They could only succeed when internal and external factors weakened even these states.


- Cultural and Religious Developments
    There was an outburst of many-sided cultural activity in India under Mughal rule. The traditions in the fields of architecture, painting, literature, and music created during this period set a norm and deeply influenced succeeding generations. In this sense, the Mughal period can be called a second classical age, following the Gupta age in northern India. In this cultural development, Indian traditions were amalgamated with the Turko-Iranian culture brought to the country by the Mughals. The Timurid court at Samarqand had developed as the cultural center of West and Central Asia. Babur was conscious of this cultural heritage. He was critical of many cultural forms existing in India and was determined to set proper standards.

    The development of art and culture in various regions of India during the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries had led to a rich and varied cultural landscape, which the Mughals could draw upon. Without this foundation, the cultural efflorescence of the Mughal age would hardly have been possible. People from different regions of India, as well as those belonging to different faiths and races, contributed to this cultural development in various ways. In this sense, the culture developed during this period tended towards a composite national culture.

    Architecture
        The Mughals built magnificent forts, palaces, gates, public buildings, mosques, and baolis (step-wells). They also laid out many formal gardens with running water. In fact, the use of running water even in their palaces and pleasure resorts was a special feature of Mughal architecture. Babur was very fond of gardens and laid out a few in the neighborhoods of Agra and Lahore. Some of the Mughal gardens, such as Nishat Bagh in Kashmir, Shalimar Bagh in Lahore, and Pinjore Garden in the Punjab foothills, have survived to this day.

        A new impetus to architecture was given by Sher Shah. His famous mausoleum at Sasaram (Bihar) and his mosque in the Old Fort at Delhi are considered architectural marvels. They mark the climax of the pre-Mughal style of architecture and the starting point for the new Mughal style.

        Akbar was the first Mughal ruler who had the time and resources to undertake large-scale construction. He built a series of forts, the most famous of which is the Agra Fort. Built in red sandstone, this massive fort had many magnificent gates. For their forts, the Mughals drew upon the developed Indian tradition of fort-building, such as the ones at Gwalior and Jodhpur. The climax of fort-building was reached in Delhi, where Shah Jahan built the famous Red Fort.

        In 1572, Akbar commenced a palace-cum-fort complex at Fatehpur Sikri, 36 kilometers from Agra, which he completed in eight years. Built atop a hill, along with a large artificial lake, it included many buildings in the styles of Gujarat and Bengal, featuring deep eaves, balconies, and ornate kiosks. The Panch Mahal, built for taking in fresh air, used various types of pillars seen in Indian temples to support its flat roofs. The Gujarat style of architecture was most widely used in the palace, probably built for his Rajput wife or wives. Similar buildings were also constructed in Agra Fort, though only a few have survived. Akbar took a keen personal interest in construction work, both at Agra and Fatehpur Sikri.

        Persian and Central Asian influences can be seen in the glazed blue tiles used for wall decoration and roofing. However, the most magnificent building was the Jama Masjid and its grand entrance, the Buland Darwaza (the “Lofty Gate”), built to commemorate Akbar’s victory in Gujarat. The gate was built in the half-dome portal style, an architectural feature borrowed from Iran. This style involved slicing a dome in half, using the sliced portion to create a massive outward facade while placing smaller doors in the rear wall where the dome and floor met. This technique became a defining feature of later Mughal architecture.

        With the consolidation of the empire, Mughal architecture reached its climax. Towards the end of Jahangir’s reign, builders began using marble instead of sandstone and decorating walls with floral designs made of semi-precious stones. This method, called pietra dura, became even more popular under Shah Jahan, who used it extensively in the Taj Mahal, a masterpiece of Mughal architecture.

        The Taj Mahal skillfully combined all the architectural forms developed by the Mughals. Humayun’s Tomb, built in Delhi at the beginning of Akbar’s reign, with its massive marble dome, is considered a precursor to the Taj. The double-dome technique, which allowed for a larger outer dome with a smaller inner dome, was another innovation of the time. The chief glory of the Taj Mahal is its massive dome and the four slender minarets connecting the platform to the main building. The decorations are kept to a minimum, with delicate marble screens, pietra dura inlay work, and chhatris (domed kiosks) adding to its grandeur. The Taj gains further beauty from its setting within a formal garden.

        Mosque-building also reached its peak under Shah Jahan. The two most noteworthy examples are:
            •	The Moti Masjid in the Agra Fort, built entirely of marble like the Taj Mahal.
            •	The Jama Masjid in Delhi, built in red sandstone, featuring a lofty entrance gate, tall slender minarets, and a series of domes.

        Although Aurangzeb was more economy-minded and did not construct many buildings, Mughal architectural traditions continued into the eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries. The Mughal influence is evident in the palaces and forts of many provincial and local kingdoms. Even the Harmandir Sahib, also known as the Golden Temple in Amritsar, was rebuilt multiple times during this period. It was constructed using the arch-and-dome principle and incorporated many elements of Mughal architecture.

    PAINTING
        The Mughals made a distinctive contribution to the field of painting.
        They introduced new themes depicting the court, battle scenes, and the chase, and added new colors and new forms. They created a living tradition of painting that continued to thrive in different parts of the country long after the glory of the Mughals had faded.

        The richness of the Mughal style was due, in part, to India’s long tradition of painting. The wall paintings of Ajanta serve as eloquent evidence of its vigor. Although the tradition seemed to decline after the eighth century, palm-leaf manuscripts and illustrated Jain texts from the thirteenth century onwards show that it had not disappeared entirely.

        Apart from the Jains, some provincial kingdoms, such as Malwa and Gujarat, extended their patronage to painting during the fifteenth century. However, a vigorous revival began only under Akbar. While at the court of the Shah of Iran, Humayun had taken into his service two master painters who accompanied him to India. Under their leadership, during Akbar’s reign, a painting workshop was set up in one of the imperial establishments (havbhanas). A large number of painters, many from the lower castes, were drawn from different parts of the country. From the beginning, both Hindus and Muslims participated in this artistic endeavor. Notable painters of Akbar’s court included Daswant and Basawan. The school developed rapidly and soon became a celebrated center of artistic production.

        Initially, Mughal painters illustrated Persian books of fables, but they were soon assigned the task of illustrating the Persian text of the Mahabharata, the historical work Akbar Nama, and others. Indian themes, scenes, and landscapes thus became popular, helping to free the Mughal school from Persian influence. Indian colors, such as peacock blue and Indian red, were incorporated. Moreover, the somewhat flat effect of the Persian style was gradually replaced by the roundedness of the Indian brush, giving the paintings a three-dimensional effect.

        Mughal painting reached its climax under Jahangir, who had a highly discerning eye for art. It became a practice in the Mughal school for different artists to paint the faces, bodies, and feet of figures in a single picture. Jahangir claimed that he could distinguish the work of each artist in a painting.

        In addition to hunting, battle, and court scenes, Jahangir’s reign saw remarkable progress in portrait painting and depictions of animals. The artist Mansur was especially renowned in this field. Portrait painting also became highly fashionable.

        Under Akbar, European painting was introduced to the Mughal court by Portuguese priests. Their influence led to the adoption of foreshortening, a technique that allowed for a more realistic representation of depth and perspective.

        Although the tradition continued under Shah Jahan, Aurangzeb’s lack of interest in painting led to the dispersal of artists to different parts of the country. This, however, contributed to the development of painting in the states of Rajasthan and the Punjab hills. The Mughal tradition of painting was revived in the eighteenth century under the patronage of Aurangzeb’s successors.

        The Rajasthani style of painting combined the themes and earlier traditions of the Western Indian or Jain school with Mughal forms and styles. In addition to hunting and court scenes, Rajasthani painting also depicted mythological themes, such as Krishna’s dalliance with Radha, the Barahmasa (seasons), and the Ragas (melodies). The Pahari school continued these artistic traditions.

    LANGUAGE, LITERATURE, AND MUSIC
        The important role of Persian and Sanskrit as vehicles of thought and government at the all-India level, and the development of regional languages, largely as a result of the growth of the Bhakti Movement, have already been mentioned. Regional languages also developed due to the patronage extended to them by local and regional rulers.

        These trends continued during the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries. By the time of Akbar, knowledge of Persian had become so widespread in North India that he dispensed with the tradition of keeping revenue records in the local language (Hindavi) in addition to Persian. However, the tradition of keeping revenue records in the local language continued in the Deccani states until their extinction in the last quarter of the seventeenth century.

        Persian prose and poetry reached a climax under Akbar’s reign. Abul Fazl, who was a great scholar and stylist, as well as the leading historian of the age, set a style of prose writing that was emulated for many generations. The leading poet of the age was his brother, Faizi, who also helped in Akbar’s translation department. The translation of the Mahabharata was carried out under his supervision. Utbi and Naziri were two other leading Persian poets. Though born in Persia, they were among the many poets and scholars who migrated from Iran to India during the period and made the Mughal court one of the cultural centers of the Islamic world. Hindus also contributed to the growth of Persian literature. Apart from literary and historical works, a number of famous dictionaries of the Persian language were also compiled during the period.

        The number of Sanskrit works produced during the period is quite impressive. As before, most of the works were produced in South and East India under the patronage of local rulers, but the main center was Banaras, where scholars from different parts of the country used to congregate. A few works were produced by Brahmins employed in the translation department of the Mughal emperors.

        Regional languages acquired stability and maturity, and some of the finest lyrical poetry was produced during this period. The dalliance of Krishna with Radha and the milkmaids, the pranks of the child Krishna, and stories from the Bhagavata Purana figure largely in lyrical poetry in Bengali, Oriya, Hindi, Rajasthani, and Gujarati during this period. Many devotional hymns to Rama were also composed, and the Ramayana and the Mahabharata were translated into regional languages, especially where they had not been translated earlier. A few translations and adaptations from Persian were also made. Both Hindus and Muslims contributed to this. Thus, Alaol composed in Bengali and also translated from Persian. In Hindi, the Padmavat, the story written by the Sufi saint Malik Muhammad Jayasi, used the attack of Alauddin Khalji on Chittor as an allegory to expound Sufi ideas on the relationship of the soul with God, along with Hindu ideas about maya.

        Medieval Hindi in the Braj form, that is, the dialect spoken in the neighborhood of Agra, was also patronized by the Mughal emperor and Hindu rulers. From the time of Akbar, Hindi poets began to be attached to the Mughal court. A leading Mughal noble, Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan, produced a fine blend of Bhakti poetry with Persian ideas of life and human relations. Thus, the Persian and Hindi literary traditions began to influence each other. But the most influential Hindi poet was Tulsidas, who venerated Rama as both a god and a hero, and he used a dialect of Hindi spoken in the eastern parts of Uttar Pradesh. Pleading for a modified caste system based not on birth but on individual qualities, Tulsidas was essentially a humanistic poet who upheld family ideals and complete devotion to Rama as a way of salvation open to all, irrespective of caste.

        In South India, Malayalam started its literary career as a separate language in its own right. Marathi reached its peak at the hands of Eknath and Tukaram. Asserting the importance of Marathi, Eknath exclaimed: “If Sanskrit was made by God, was Prakrit born of thieves and knaves? Let these errings be of vanity alone. God is no partisan of tongues. To Him, Prakrit and Sanskrit are alike. My language, Marathi, is worthy of expressing the highest sentiments and is rich, laden with the fruits of divine knowledge.”

        This undoubtedly expresses the sentiments of all those writing in local languages. It also shows the confidence and the status acquired by these languages. Due to the writings of the Sikh Gurus, Punjabi received new life.

        MUSIC
            Another branch of cultural life in which Hindus and Muslims cooperated was music. Akbar patronized Tansen of Gwalior, who is credited with composing many new melodies (ragas). Jahangir and Shah Jahan, as well as many Mughal nobles, followed this example. There are many stories about the banning of music by the orthodox Aurangzeb. Recent research shows that Aurangzeb banished singing from his court but not the playing of musical instruments. In fact, Aurangzeb himself was an accomplished veena player. Music in all forms continued to be patronized by Aurangzeb’s queens in the harem and by princes and nobles. That is why the largest number of books on classical Indian music in Persian were written during Aurangzeb’s reign. However, some of the most important developments in the field of music took place later in the eighteenth century during the reign of Muhammad Shah (1719–1748), who was a great patron of music and musicians.

    Religious Ideas, Beliefs, and Problems of Integration
        The Bhakti Movement continued to flourish during the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries. Among the new movements was the Sikh movement in Punjab and what is called Maharashtra Dharma in Maharashtra. The Sikh movement originated with the preachings of Guru Nanak, but its development is closely linked with the institution of Guruship. The first four gurus continued the tradition of quiet meditation and scholarship. The fifth guru, Guru Arjan, completed the compilation of the Sikh scriptures called the Adi Granth or Granth Sahib. To emphasize that the guru combined both spiritual and worldly leadership, Guru Arjan began to live in an aristocratic style. He erected lofty buildings in Amritsar, wore fine clothes, procured fine horses from Central Asia, and maintained retainers in attendance. He also introduced a system of collecting offerings from the Sikhs at the rate of one-tenth of their income to promote the movement.

        Akbar had been deeply impressed with the Sikh gurus and is said to have visited them at Amritsar. However, a conflict arose when Guru Arjan was imprisoned and executed by Jahangir on the charge of aiding the rebel prince Khusrau with money and prayers. His successor, Guru Hargobind, was also imprisoned for a time but was soon released and developed good relations with Jahangir, even accompanying him on his journey to Kashmir before the emperor’s death. Guru Hargobind later clashed with Shah Jahan over a hunting incident. While the emperor was hunting near Amritsar, one of his favorite hawks flew into the guru’s camp, and his refusal to return it led to a series of skirmishes. However, the matter was settled through court intervention.

        A second conflict arose when the guru’s attempt to build a new city on the river Beas near Jalandhar was objected to. A third conflict occurred when two horses of exceptional beauty and speed, being brought to the guru from Central Asia, were seized by royal officials. A follower of the guru, Bidhi Chand, stole these horses and presented them to the guru. By this time, the guru had a significant following, and in a series of battles, he proved himself a capable leader. The guru was supported for some time by a Pathan, Painda Khan. Ultimately, Guru Hargobind retired to the Punjab foothills and was left undisturbed.

        According to the historian R. S. Tripathi, these conflicts were of an “inconsequential nature” and were driven more by personal and political factors than religious differences. The guru’s assumption of a rich lifestyle and the title Sachcha Padshah or “True Sovereign” did not seem to trouble the Mughal rulers, as similar practices were observed among some Sufi saints who were also given grand titles by their followers to emphasize their spiritual eminence.

        There was no atmosphere of confrontation between the Sikhs and the Mughal rulers during this period. Nor was there any systematic persecution of Hindus, and hence, no occasion for the Sikhs or any sect to emerge as the champions of Hindus against religious oppression. Despite Shah Jahan displaying orthodoxy at the beginning of his reign and engaging in a few acts of intolerance, such as demolishing newly built temples, he was not rigid in his outlook. His policies became more tempered towards the end of his reign under the influence of his liberal son, Dara Shikoh. Dara, the eldest son of Shah Jahan, was a scholar and Sufi who enjoyed discussing religious matters with learned men. With the help of Brahmans from Kashi, he translated the Bhagavad Gita into Persian. However, his most significant work was an anthology of the Vedas, in the introduction to which he declared them to be “heavenly books in point of time” and “in conformity with the Holy Quran.” Through this, he emphasized the idea that there were no fundamental differences between Hinduism and Islam.

        Another saint, Dadu, who was born in Gujarat but lived mostly in Rajasthan, preached a non-sectarian path (Nirpaksha). He refused to identify himself with either Hindus or Muslims or adhere to the revealed scriptures of either religion, asserting the indivisibility of Brahma or the Supreme Reality.

        A similar trend of religious tolerance was evident in the life and works of Tukaram, the supreme exponent of Bhakti in Maharashtra. He lived in Pandharpur, which had become the center of Maharashtra Dharma and the worship of Vithoba, a form of Vishnu. Tukaram, who claimed to have been born a Shudra, personally performed puja to the deity.

        It was not expected that such ideas and practices would be easily accepted by orthodox elements of both Hinduism and Islam, as they would have had to relinquish their entrenched positions of power and influence. The sentiments of orthodox Hindus were echoed by Raghunandan of Navadwipa (Nadia) in Bengal, who was considered the most influential writer on Dharmashastras during the medieval period. Raghunandan asserted the privileges of Brahmins, stating that only Brahmins had the right to read scriptures and preach. He further declared that in the Kali Yuga, there were only two varnas: Brahmins and Shudras, as true Kshatriyas had long disappeared, and the Vaishyas and others had lost their caste status due to the neglect of their prescribed duties. Ramdas of Maharashtra, who later became the spiritual guru of Shivaji and promoted a philosophy of activism, also strongly asserted Brahminical privileges.

        Among Muslims, while the trend of Sufism and religious harmony continued, a small group of orthodox ulama opposed it, along with Akbar’s liberal policies. The most renowned figure in the Muslim orthodox and revivalist movement was Shaikh Ahmad Sirhindi. A follower of the orthodox Naqshbandi school of Sufis, which had been introduced in India during Akbar’s reign, Shaikh Ahmad Sirhindi opposed the concept of pantheistic mysticism (Wahdat al-Wujud or unity of God and the created being), denouncing it as un-Islamic. He also opposed all practices and beliefs that he believed stemmed from Hindu influence, such as the use of music in religious gatherings (sama), excessive meditation, and visiting the tombs of saints. To assert the Islamic character of the state, he demanded the reimposition of jizya, a stricter stance against Hindus, and minimal association between Muslims and Hindus. To promote his agenda, he established centers and wrote letters to the emperor and nobles in an attempt to gain their support.

        However, Shaikh Ahmad’s ideas had little impact. Jahangir imprisoned him for claiming a status beyond that of the Prophet and only released him after his retraction. Aurangzeb, too, paid little attention to his successors.

        Thus, the influence of orthodox thinkers and preachers remained limited, confined to narrow circles. Their chief hope lay in gaining the support of those in power and wealth, while the liberal thinkers sought to appeal to the broader masses.

        The recurrent cycles of liberalism and orthodoxy in Indian history must be understood within the context of Indian society. One aspect of this struggle was between entrenched privilege and power on one hand and the egalitarian and humanistic aspirations of the people on the other.

        The influence of narrow, orthodox elements and their attempts to reassert rigid beliefs posed a barrier to growing reconciliation and tolerance between the followers of Hinduism and Islam. This struggle for religious and cultural integration reached a climax during Aurangzeb’s reign. However, the broad, liberal religious policies of Akbar were revived in the eighteenth century and became the norm once again.


- Climax and Disintegration of the Mughal Empire—I

    Problems of Succession
        The last years of Shah Jahan’s reign were clouded by a bitter war of succession among his sons. There was no clear tradition of succession among the Muslims or the Timurids. The right of nomination by the ruler had been accepted by some Muslim political thinkers, but it could not be asserted. The Timurid tradition of partitioning had not been successful either and was never applied in India.

        Hindu traditions were also not very clear in the matter of succession. According to Tulsidas, a contemporary of Akbar, a ruler had the right to give the gaddi to any one of his sons. However, there were many cases among the Rajputs where such a nomination was not accepted by the other brothers. For instance, Sanga had to wage a bitter struggle with his brothers before he could assert his claim to the throne.

        Towards the end of 1657, Shah Jahan fell ill in Delhi, and for some time, his life was despaired of. However, he gradually recovered under the loving care of Dara. Meanwhile, all kinds of rumors spread. It was said that Shah Jahan had already died and that Dara was concealing the truth to serve his own purposes. After some time, Shah Jahan slowly made his way by boat to Agra. Meanwhile, the princes—Shuja in Bengal, Murad in Gujarat, and Aurangzeb in the Deccan—had either been persuaded that these rumors were true or pretended to believe them and began making preparations for the inevitable war of succession.

        Anxious to avert a conflict between his sons, which could spell ruin for the empire, and anticipating his own end, Shah Jahan decided to nominate his eldest son, Dara, as his successor (tvadi-ahd). He raised Dara’s mansab from 40,000 zat to the unprecedented rank of 60,000. Dara was given a chair next to the throne, and all nobles were instructed to obey him as their future sovereign. However, instead of ensuring a smooth succession, as Shah Jahan had hoped, these actions convinced the other princes of Shah Jahan’s partiality toward Dara. This strengthened their resolve to make a bid for the throne.

        It is not necessary to follow in detail the events leading to the ultimate triumph of Aurangzeb. There were many reasons for Aurangzeb’s success. Divided counsel and underestimation of his opponents by Dara were two major factors responsible for his defeat. Upon hearing of the military preparations of his sons and their decision to march on the capital, Shah Jahan sent an army to the east led by Dara’s son, Sulaiman Shikoh, aided by Mirza Raja Jai Singh, to deal with Shuja, who had crowned himself. Another army was sent to Malwa under Raja Jaswant Singh, the ruler of Jodhpur. Upon arriving in Malwa, Jaswant found himself facing the combined forces of Aurangzeb and Murad. The two princes were intent on battle and invited Jaswant to stand aside. Jaswant could have retreated, but considering it a matter of dishonor, he decided to stand and fight, though the odds were against him. The victory of Aurangzeb at Dharmat (15 April 1658) emboldened his supporters and raised his prestige while discouraging Dara and his allies.

        Meanwhile, Dara made a serious mistake. Overconfident in his position, he had assigned some of his best troops to the eastern campaign. Thus, he weakened the defense of Agra. Led by Sulaiman Shikoh, the army moved east and performed well. It surprised and defeated Shuja near Banaras (February 1658) and then pursued him into Bihar—as if the issue at Agra had already been decided. After the defeat at Dharmat, urgent messages were sent to these forces to hurry back to Agra. After hastily concluding a treaty (7 May 1658), Sulaiman Shikoh started his march to Agra from his camp near Monghyr in eastern Bihar. However, it was unlikely that he would reach Agra in time to confront Aurangzeb.

        After Dharmat, Dara desperately sought allies. He sent repeated letters to Jaswant Singh, who had retired to Jodhpur. The Rana of Udaipur was also approached. Jaswant Singh moved out slowly to Pushkar near Ajmer. After raising an army with money provided by Dara, he waited for the Rana to join him. However, the Rana had already been won over by Aurangzeb with promises of a rank of 7,000 and the return of territories seized by Shah Jahan and Dara in 1654 over a dispute regarding the fortification of Chittor. Aurangzeb also assured the Rana of religious freedom and favors equal to those given to Rana Sanga. Thus, Dara failed to win over even the important Rajput rajas to his side.

        The Battle of Samugarh (29 May 1658) was essentially a contest of generalship, with both sides being almost evenly matched (about 50,000 to 60,000 troops each). In this field, Dara was no match for Aurangzeb. The Hada Rajputs and the Saiyids of Barha, upon whom Dara heavily depended, could not compensate for the weaknesses of the hastily recruited army. Aurangzeb’s troops were battle-hardened and well-led.

        Aurangzeb had always pretended that his only reason for coming to Agra was to see his ailing father and free him from the control of the “heretical” Dara. However, the war between Aurangzeb and Dara was not a contest between religious orthodoxy and liberalism. Both Muslim and Hindu nobles were divided in their support for the two rivals. The attitude of the nobles depended on their personal interests and affiliations with individual princes.

        After Dara’s defeat and flight, Shah Jahan was besieged in the Agra Fort. Aurangzeb forced Shah Jahan to surrender by cutting off the fort’s water supply. Shah Jahan was confined to the female apartments in the fort and strictly supervised, though he was not mistreated. He lived there for eight years, lovingly cared for by his favorite daughter, Jahanara, who voluntarily chose to stay with him. She re-entered public life after Shah Jahan’s death and was highly honored by Aurangzeb, who restored her to the position of the first lady of the realm and increased her annual pension from twelve lakh rupees to seventeen lakhs.

        According to the terms of Aurangzeb’s agreement with Murad, the kingdom was to be divided between them. However, Aurangzeb had no intention of sharing power. He treacherously imprisoned Murad and sent him to the Gwalior jail, where he was killed two years later.

        After losing the battle at Samugarh, Dara fled to Lahore, hoping to retain control of the surrounding areas. However, Aurangzeb soon arrived with a strong army. Dara lost courage, abandoned Lahore without a fight, and fled to Sindh, thereby sealing his fate. Although the civil war dragged on for more than two years, its outcome was never in doubt. Dara’s move from Sindh into Gujarat, then to Ajmer on an invitation from Jaswant Singh, and the latter’s subsequent betrayal are well-known. The Battle of Deorai near Ajmer (March 1659) was the last major battle Dara fought against Aurangzeb. Dara could have escaped to Iran, but he chose to try his luck again in Afghanistan. On the way, in the Bolan Pass, a treacherous Afghan chief captured him and handed him over to Aurangzeb. A panel of jurists ruled that Dara could not be allowed to live “to protect the faith and holy law and for reasons of state.” This illustrates how Aurangzeb used religion as a cover for his political ambitions. Two years later, Dara’s son, Sulaiman Shikoh, who had sought refuge with the ruler of Garhwal, was handed over to Aurangzeb under the threat of invasion. He met the same fate as his father.

        Earlier, Aurangzeb had defeated Shuja at Khajwah near Allahabad (December 1658). Further campaigning against him was entrusted to Mir Jumla, who steadily exerted pressure until Shuja was hounded out of India into Arakan (April 1660). Soon afterward, he and his family met a dishonorable death at the hands of the Arakanese on a charge of fomenting rebellion.

        The civil war, which kept the empire distracted for more than two years, showed that neither nomination by the ruler nor plans for the division of the empire were likely to be accepted by the contenders for the throne. Military force became the only arbiter for succession, and the civil wars became steadily more destructive. After being seated securely on the throne, Aurangzeb tried to mitigate, to some extent, the effects of the harsh Mughal custom of war unto death between brothers. At the instance of Jahanara Begum, Siphir Shikoh, son of Dara, was released from prison in 1673, given a mansab, and married to a daughter of Aurangzeb. Murad’s son, Izzat Bakhsh, was also released, given a mansab, and married to another daughter of Aurangzeb. Earlier, in 1669, Dara’s daughter, Jani Begum, who had been looked after by Jahanara as her own daughter, was married to Aurangzeb’s third son, Muhammad Azam. There were many other marriages between Aurangzeb’s family and the children and grandchildren of his defeated brothers. Thus, in the third generation, the families of Aurangzeb and his defeated brothers became one.

    Aurangzeb's Reign—His Religious Policy
        Aurangzeb ruled for almost 50 years. During his long reign, the Mughal Empire reached its territorial climax. At its height, it stretched from Kashmir in the north to Jinji in the south, and from the Hindukush in the west to Chittagong in the east. Aurangzeb proved to be a hardworking ruler and never spared himself or his subordinates in the tasks of government. His letters show the close attention he paid to all affairs of the state. He was a strict disciplinarian who did not spare his own sons. In 1686, he imprisoned Prince Muazzam on a charge of intriguing with the ruler of Golconda and kept him in prison for 12 long years. His other sons also had to face his wrath on various occasions. Such was the awe of Aurangzeb that even late in his life when Muazzam was the Governor of Kabul, he trembled every time he received a letter from his father, who was then in South India. Unlike his predecessors, Aurangzeb did not like ostentation. His personal life was marked by simplicity. He had the reputation of being an orthodox, God-fearing Muslim. In the course of time, he began to be regarded as a zinda pir, or 'a living saint.'

        Historians are, however, deeply divided about Aurangzeb's achievements as a ruler. According to some, he reversed Akbar's policy of religious toleration and thus undermined the loyalty of the Hindus to the empire. According to them, this, in turn, led to popular uprisings which sapped the vitality of the empire. His suspicious nature added to his problems so that, in the words of Khafi Khan, "all his enterprises were long drawn out" and ended in failure. Another set of historians thinks that Aurangzeb has been unjustly maligned, that the Hindus had become disloyal due to the laxity of Aurangzeb's predecessors, so that Aurangzeb had no option but to adopt harsh methods and to try to rally the Muslims, on whose support, in the long run, the empire had to rest.

        A new trend has, however, emerged, as shown in the research work on Aurangzeb. In these works, efforts have been made to assess Aurangzeb's political and religious policies in the context of social, economic, and institutional developments. There is little doubt about his being orthodox in his beliefs. He was not interested in philosophical debates or in mysticism, though he did occasionally visit Sufi saints for their blessings, and did not debar his sons from dabbling in Sufism. While taking his stand on the Hanafi school of Muslim law, which had been traditionally followed in India, Aurangzeb did not hesitate in issuing secular decrees, called zawabit. A compendium of his decrees, and government rules and regulations, was collected in a work called Zawabit-i-Alamgiri. Theoretically, the zawabit supplemented the sharia. In practice, however, they sometimes modified the sharia, in view of the conditions obtaining in India which were not provided for in the sharia.

        Thus, apart from being an orthodox Muslim, Aurangzeb was also a ruler. He could hardly forget the political reality that the overwhelming population of India was Hindu and that they were deeply attached to their faith. Any policy which meant the complete alienation of the Hindus and of the powerful Hindu rajas and zamindars was obviously unworkable.

        In analyzing Aurangzeb's religious policy, we may take note first of what have been called moral and religious regulations. At the beginning of his reign, he forbade the Kalma from being inscribed on coins lest a coin be trampled underfoot or be defiled while passing from hand to hand. He discontinued the festival of Nauroz as it was considered a Zoroastrian practice favored by the Safavid rulers of Iran. Muhtasibs were appointed in all the provinces. These officials were asked to see that people lived their lives in accordance with the sharia. Thus, it was the business of these officials to see that wine and intoxicants such as bhang were not consumed in public places. They were also responsible for regulating houses of ill repute, gambling dens, etc., and for checking weights and measures. In other words, they were responsible for ensuring that things forbidden by the sharia and the zawabit (secular decrees) were, as far as possible, not flouted openly. In appointing muhtasibs, Aurangzeb emphasized that the state was also responsible for the moral welfare of the citizens, especially the Muslims. But these officials were instructed not to interfere in the private lives of citizens.

        Later, in the eleventh year of his reign (1669), Aurangzeb took a number of measures which have been called puritanical. They were designed to show that the emperor was opposed to all practices which were not in accordance with the sharia, or which could be considered superstitious. Some measures were of an economic and social nature. Thus, he forbade singing in the court and the official musicians were pensioned off. Instrumental music and naubat (the royal band), however, continued. Singing also continued to be patronized by the ladies in the haram, and by princes and individual nobles. It is of some interest to note, as has been mentioned before, that the largest number of Persian works on classical Indian music were written in Aurangzeb's reign, and that Aurangzeb himself was proficient in playing the veena. Thus, the jibe of Aurangzeb to the protesting musicians that they should bury the beat of music they were carrying deep under the earth so "that no echo of it may rise again" was only an angry remark.

        Aurangzeb discontinued the practice of jharokha darshan or showing himself to the public from the balcony since he considered it a superstitious practice and against Islam. Similarly, he forbade the ceremony of weighing the emperor against gold and silver and other articles on his birthdays. This practice, which was apparently started during Akbar's reign, had become widespread and had become a burden on the smaller nobles. But the weight of social opinion was too much; Aurangzeb had to permit this ceremony for his sons when they recovered from illness. He forbade astrologers to prepare almanacs. But the order was flouted by everybody, including members of the royal family.

        Many other regulations of a similar nature, some of a moral character and some to instill a sense of austerity, were issued. The throne room was to be furnished in a cheap and simple style; clerks were to use porcelain ink-stands instead of silver ones; silk clothes were frowned upon; the gold railings in the diwan-i-am were replaced by those of lapis lazuli set on gold. Even the official department of history-writing was discontinued as a measure of economy.

        To promote trade among the Muslims who depended almost exclusively on state support, Aurangzeb at first largely exempted Muslim traders from the payment of cess on the import of goods. But he soon found that the Muslim traders were abusing it, even passing off the goods of Hindu merchants as their own to cheat the state. So Aurangzeb re-imposed the cess on Muslim traders but kept it at half of what was charged from others.

        Similarly, he tried to reserve the posts of peshkars and karoris (petty revenue officials) for Muslims but soon had to modify it in the face of opposition from the nobles and lack of qualified Muslims.

        We may now turn our attention to some of the other measures of Aurangzeb, which may be called discriminatory and showed a sense of bigotry towards people professing other religions. The most important was Aurangzeb’s attitude towards temples and the levying of jizyah.

        At the outset of his reign, Aurangzeb reiterated the position of the sharia regarding temples, synagogues, churches, etc., that ‘long-standing temples should not be demolished, but no new temples allowed to be built.’ Further, old places of worship could be repaired ‘since buildings cannot last forever.’ This position is clearly spelled out in a number of extant farmans he issued to the Brahmans of Banaras, Vrindavan, etc.

        Aurangzeb’s order regarding temples was not a new one. It reaffirmed the position that had existed during the Sultanate period and had been reiterated by Shah Jahan early in his reign. In practice, it left wide latitude to the local officials regarding the interpretation of the term ‘long-standing temples.’ The private opinion and sentiment of the ruler in the matter were also bound to weigh with the officials. For example, after the rise of the liberal-minded Dara as Shah Jahan’s favorite, few temples had been demolished in pursuance of his order forbidding new temples. Aurangzeb, as governor of Gujarat, ordered a number of temples in Gujarat to be destroyed. In many cases, it meant disfiguring the images and closing down the temples. At the outset of his reign, Aurangzeb found that images in many of these temples had been restored and idol worship had resumed. Aurangzeb, therefore, ordered again in 1665 that these temples be destroyed. The famous temple of Somnath, which he ordered to be destroyed earlier in his reign, was apparently one of the temples mentioned above.

        The Banaras farman is in the National Library, Calcutta, and the Vrindavan farman is presently in a temple at Jaipur.

        It does not seem that Aurangzeb’s order regarding the ban on new temples led to a large-scale destruction of temples at the outset of his reign. Later, as Aurangzeb encountered political opposition from a number of quarters, such as the Marathas, Jats, etc., he seems to have considered it legitimate to destroy even long-standing Hindu temples as a measure of punishment and as a warning. Further, he began to look upon temples as centers of spreading subversive ideas, that is, ideas that were not acceptable to the orthodox elements. Thus, he took strict action when he learned in 1669 that in some of the temples in Thatta, Multan, and especially at Banaras, both Hindus and Muslims used to come from great distances to learn from the Brahmans. Aurangzeb issued orders to the governors of all provinces to put down such practices and to destroy the temples where such practices took place. As a result of these orders, a number of temples, such as the famous temple of Vishwanath at Banaras and the temple of Keshava Rai at Mathura built by Bir Singh Deo Bundela in the reign of Jahangir, were destroyed and mosques erected in their place. The destruction of these temples had a political motive as well. Mustaid Khan, author of the Maasir-i-Alamgiri, says, with reference to the destruction of the temple of Keshava Rai at Mathura, ‘On seeing this instance of the strength of the Emperor's faith and the grandeur of his devotion to God, the proud rajas were stifled, and in amazement, they stood like images facing the wall.’

        It was in this context that many temples built in Orissa during the last ten to twelve years were also destroyed. But it does not seem that there were any orders for the general destruction of temples. Mustaid Khan, who wrote his history of Aurangzeb in the early eighteenth century and who had been closely associated with Aurangzeb, asserts that the motive of Aurangzeb’s orders was to ‘establish Islam’ and that the Emperor ordered the governors to destroy all temples and to ban the public practice of the religion of these misbelievers, that is, the Hindus. If Mustaid Khan’s version were correct, it would have meant Aurangzeb going far beyond the position of the sharia, for the sharia did not ban non-Muslims from practicing their faiths as long as they were loyal to the ruler.
